On the irreducible components of some Brill-Noether loci of rank-two, stable bundles over a general ν \nu -gonal curve Thanks: The first author was supported by the National Research Foundation of Korea(NRF) grant funded by the Korea government(MSIT) (RS-2024-00352592). The third author was supported by the National Research Foundation of Korea(NRF) grant funded by the Korea government(MSIT) (2022R1A2C100597713). First and third authors also benefitted of support by research funds “AAAGH-CUP E83C22001710005” (Responsible Prof. G. Pareschi) of the University of Rome Tor Vergata, during their visit at the Department on Mathematics in November 2023, when collaboration began. The second author acknowledges the support of the MIUR Excellence Department Project MatMod@TOV, MIUR CUP-E83C23000330006, 2023-2027, awarded to the Department of Mathematics, University of Rome Tor Vergata, and the support from the Organizers of the conference “Workshop in Classical Algebraic Geometry”, Daejeon-South Korea, 12-15 December 2023 (Prof. D. S. Hwang, J.-M. Hwang, Y. Lee) as an invited speaker, occasion in which this collaboration has been further developed; moreover he is member of INdAM-GNSAGA
Youngook Choi, Flaminio Flamini, Seonja Kim

Abstract
. In this paper we consider Brill-Noether loci of rank-two, stable vector bundles of given degree d d , with speciality 2 2 and 3 3 , on a general ν \nu -gonal curve C C of genus g g with the aim of studying their irreducible components in the whole range of interest for d d , namely 2 ​ g − 2 ≤ d ≤ 4 ​ g − 4 2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4 as from ( 1.17 ) below. For speciality 2 2 , either we prove that such a Brill-Noether locus is empty or we completely classify all of its irreducible components, giving also some extra information about their dimensions (exhibiting both regular and superabundant components), about their birational geometry as well as giving precise description of their general point. Similarly, in speciality 3 3 , either we prove that such a Brill-Noether locus is empty or we exhibit irreducible components of several types according to their regularity or superabundance but also according to the precise descriptions of their general points, proving e.g. that in some degrees d d there are more than one superabundant component together with the presence of also a regular component. We moreover give extra information about the birational geometry of the constructed components as well as about their local behavior. As a by-product of our general results, we deduce also some consequences on Brill-Noether loci in rank two with fixed general determinant line bundle instead of fixed degree.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
14H60, 14H10, 14D20, 14J26, 14E05

Introduction

Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on smooth, complex, projective curves
C
of genus
g
is
a fundamental topic in Algebraic Geometry, whose study goes back (at least) to Riemann in 1851
[
38
]
, and which addresses the problem of determining which
invariants
are needed to understand the behavior of maps from a smooth, projective curve
C
to a projective space. This set-up basically deals with determining the geometry of the
space of maps

C\to\mathbb{P}^{r}
, with
r>0
and of given positive degree
d
.

The
discrete invariants

(g,r,d)
are equivalent to the datum of line bundles
L
on
C
of genus
g
, which are of degree
d
and which are equipped with
(r+1)
-dimensional base-point-free vector spaces of global sections. Therefore investigation on the geometry of such maps is related to the study of (locally determinantal) subschemes of
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
, called
Brill-Noether loci

W_{d}^{r}(C)
, set-theoretically defined as:

W_{d}^{r}(C):=\left\{L\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)\;|\;h^{0}(C,L)\geq r+1\right\}\subseteq{\rm Pic}^{d}(C).

When
C
is a
general
genus-
g
curve, i.e.
C
with
general moduli
(namely, when
C
corresponds to a general point of the
moduli space

\mathcal{M}_{g}
parametrizing isomorphism classes of such curves), Brill-Noether loci are well-understood by main theorems from the 1970-1980’s (due to several authors as D. Eisenbud-J. Harris,  W. Fulton-R. Lazarsfeld, D. Gieseker, P. Griffiths-J. Harris, G. Kempf, S. Kleiman-D. Laksov, K. Petri, just to mention a few) which give good descriptions of the geometry of such
W^{r}_{d}(C)
’s, cf. e.g.
[
2
]
. Some properties, e.g. non-emptyness and smoothness, are of local nature whereas some others, e.g. connectedness or irreducibility, require a global understanding of the Brill-Noether locus. The core of the aforementioned results enstablishes that properties of Brill-Noether loci are quite uniform when
C
is with general moduli and that they are basically determined by the aforementioned discrete invariants
(g,r,d)
(cf.
[
2
]
for details).

Nevertheless some projective curves
C
can be already equipped with a (maybe
“atypical”
) map
C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{k}
of some given degree, thus it is natural to ask how the presence of such a map
f
, which may e.g. force
C
to be not general (i.e. a curve with
special moduli
), affects the properties of Brill-Noether loci
W^{r}_{d}(C)
related to maps
C\to\mathbb{P}^{r}
to be studied. The simplest example occurs when
k=1
and
\mbox{deg}(f)=\nu
with
\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
(the integer
\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
being the so called
general gonality
for genus-
g
curves, i.e. the gonality of a curve of genus
g
with general moduli), and when
C
is
general
among genus-
g
curves possessing a degree-
\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
map
f
onto
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The term “general” in this context can be made more precise by considering the
Hurwitz scheme

\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}
, which is an irreducible scheme parametrizing branched covers
C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
\nu
and genus
g
, and which admits a natural
modular map

\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{M}_{g}
, given by forgetting the maps
f
. From above, when
\nu\geq\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
, then the map
\Phi
is dominant and this is the reason why one restricts to
\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
; in such a case, one refers to
C
to be a
general
\nu
-gonal
curve of genus
g
when
C
corresponds to a general point of
{\rm Im}(\Phi)
. In this set-up, one can similarly ask about properties of Brill-Noether loci
W^{r}_{d}(C)
’s. However, in such a case, the uniform picture for Brill-Noether theory for general curves completely breaks down; for
\nu=2
and
3
there are classical results, the first case of
hyperelliptic
curves is due to Clifford
[
13
]
in 1878, whereas the second for
trigonal
curves, has been studied by Maroni
[
32
]
in 1946. For arbitrary special gonality
\nu
, some preliminary results have been given by e.g.
[
4
,
15
,
16
,
24
,
25
,
33
]
, just to mention a few and, in more recent years, there has been a great deal of interest in this topic which produced many breakthrough results (cf. e.g.
[
14
,
22
,
23
,
29
,
30
,
36
]
) showing that, differently from the case with general moduli, discrete invariants
(g,r,d)
are not enough to study Brill-Noether theory on a general
\nu
-gonal curve. In §
1.1
we will remind the most general results in this direction, which will be sometimes used later on, following in particular terminology and results by H. Larson as in
[
30
]
.

The present paper is instead related to Brill-Noether theory of rank-two, stable vector bundles of degree
d
on a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
.

More generally, for any smooth, irreducible projective curve
C
of genus
g
, one denotes by
U_{C}(n,d)
the
moduli space
of (semi)stable, degree
d
, rank-
n
vector bundles on
C
and by
U^{s}_{C}(n,d)
the open, dense subset of stable bundles. Similarly as in the line-bundle case, one can define a (locally determinantal) subscheme
B^{k}_{n,d}\subseteq U_{C}(n,d)
, cf. Remark
1.10
below, called as above
Brill-Noether locus
, which parametrizes
S
-equivalence
classes of rank-
n
vector bundles
\mathcal{F}

having
h^{0}(C,\mathcal{F})\geq k
, for a given positive integer
k
(observe that this notation is slightly different from the line-bundle case, where
B^{k+1}_{1,d}
stands for
W^{k}_{d}(C)
). The schemes
B^{k}_{n,d}
, being as natural generalizations of the Brill-Noether loci of line bundles, have received wide attention from various authors. Indeed, Brill-Noether theory for higher-rank vector bundles is a very active research area and, apart from its intrinsic interest, it is also important in view of several applications to other topics in Algebraic Geometry, like e.g. birational geometry of several moduli spaces (cf. e.g.
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
21
,
34
,
35
]
). Nonetheless, in contrast with extensive results in the line bundle case, several basic questions concerning higher-rank Brill–Noether loci, like e.g non-emptiness, dimension, irreducibility, local structure, etc., are still open in general. Furthermore, contrary to the rank-one case, Brill-Noether loci do not always behave as expected even when
C
is with general moduli (cf. e.g.
[
8
]
and Remark
1.10
-(b) below).

In this paper we will focus on the case of rank
n=2
so, to ease notation, we will simply put

U_{C}(d):=U_{C}(2,d),\;\;\;U^{s}_{C}(d):=U^{s}_{C}(2,d)\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;B^{k}_{d}:=B^{k}_{2,d}.

From standard facts related to Serre duality, preservation of (semi)stability under either dualization or tensorization by a line bundle, it turns out that in rank two it makes sense to consider non-trivial Brill-Noether loci when

2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;k=k_{i}:=d-2g+2+i,

for
i\geq 1
an integer (cf. Proposition
1.8
, formulas (
1.17
) and (
1.18
) below). In this case, it is well-known that the
expected dimension
of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(2,d)
is

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}:=4g-3-ik_{i},

which is the so-called
Brill-Noether number
in rank two (cf.
[
42
]
and (
1.20
), Remark
1.10
below). Contrary to the line bundle case, this is no longer true for possible components of
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
in
U_{C}(d)\setminus U^{s}_{C}(d)
(i.e. not containing stable points), which can occur only for
d
even (cf. Remark
1.10
for more details). Recall finally that an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}
is said to be
regular
, if it is generically smooth with expected dimension
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
,
superabundant
, otherwise (cf. Definition
1.11
below).

In the above range
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
, the Brill-Noether locus
B^{k_{1}}_{d}
has been deeply studied on any smooth, projective curve
C
(cf. e.g.
[
42
,
31
]
). Concerning
B^{k_{2}}_{d}
, using degeneration arguments, N. Sundaram
[
42
]
proved that
B^{k_{2}}_{d}
is not empty for any
C
and for odd
d
such that
2g-1\leq d\leq 3g-4
. M. Teixidor I Bigas generalized Sundaram’s result as follows:

Theorem 0.1
.

Let
C
be a non-singular, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 3
.

(i) If
d
is an integer such that
2g-3\leq d\leq 4g-7
, then for any
C
one has that
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
contains a component
\mathcal{B}
of (expected) dimension
\rho^{k_{2}}_{d}=8g-2d-11
and a general point
[\mathcal{F}]
on it corresponds to a rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
of degree
d
, whose space of sections
H^{0}(C,\mathcal{F})
has dimension
k_{2}:=d-2g+4
. If moreover
C
is with general moduli, this is the only component of
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. Moreover,
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
has extra components if and only if
W^{1}_{n}(C)
is non-empty with
\operatorname{dim}W^{1}_{n}(C)\geq 2g+2n-d-5
, for some integer
n
such that
2n<4g-4-d
.

(ii) If in particular
C
is with general moduli, the singular locus of
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
consists of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
and of the
(2g-6)
-dimensional irreducible sets
F_{b,a}
, where
F_{b,a}
is the set whose general point is a stable vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
which can be written as an extension

0\to B+A^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-A-B)\to 0,

where
B
is a divisor of degree
b
with two-sections,
A
is an effective divisor of degree
a
,
A^{\prime}
is an effective divisor,
where
b\geq\frac{g}{2}+1
and
b+a<\frac{4g-4-d}{2}
. In particular, if
d<\frac{10}{3}g-6
,
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is either empty or irreducible, of the expected dimension
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
and a general bundle in
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
has exactly
k_{3}:=d-2g+5
linearly independent global sections.

Theorem
0.1
-(i) above is the “Serre-dual” version of
[
43
, Theorem]
, i.e. the bundle
\mathcal{F}
in the statement above is
\mathcal{F}:=\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
, where
\mathcal{E}
stands for the bundle as in the proof of Theorem
0.1
in
[
43
]
, so
h^{j}(C,\mathcal{F})=h^{1-j}(C,\mathcal{E})
,
0\leq j\leq 1
. Similarly, Theorem
0.1
-(ii) is the “Serre-dual” version of
[
44
, Theorems 1 and 2]
.

Inspired by the previous results, which basically deal with curves with general moduli, the aim of this paper is to study and, when possible, classify all the irreducible components of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, for
i=2,3
, when
C
is assumed to be a general
\nu
-gonal curve, with
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
, in particular of genus
g\geq 4
. The principles we use to approach such an investigation consist in determining suitable
“geometric descriptions”
of the (isomorphism classes of) bundles
\mathcal{F}
corresponding to general points of possible irreducible components of
B^{k_{i}}_{C}(d)\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
on
C
a general
\nu
-gonal curve, for
2\leq i\leq 3
. By
“geometric descriptions”
we mean description which makes use of families of unisecant curves on the surface scroll (or ruled surface)
F:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
, which in turn translates into exhibiting the bundle
\mathcal{F}
as an extension of line bundles:

(*)\;\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to L\to 0,

which we call a
presentation
of
\mathcal{F}
, with
L
and
N
suitable line bundles on
C
. For the construction of irreducible components of some Brill-Noether locus, sometimes it will be of particular interest a presentation as in
(*)
with suitable
minimality properties
satisfied by the quotient line bundle
L
. This motivates why Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on a general
\nu
-gonal curve as in §
1.1
enters also into the game.

The approach of viewing rank-
2
vector bundles
\mathcal{F}
on
C
as extensions of line bundles is classical, going back at least to C. Segre
[
39
]
. In more recent times it has been exploited, by e.g. A. Bertram and B. Feinberg in
[
8
, § 2,3]
, S. Mukai in
[
35
, § 8]
and
[
12
]
, when
C
is a curve with general moduli. As explicitely noted by A. Bertram and B. Feinberg in
[
8
]
, such an approach works well enough in low genera but it becomes harder to implement in general. Nevertheless, we follow this route with no upper-bounds on the genus, instead by bounding the speciality of the bundle
i:=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})
up to
3
.

As we will see below, this strategy allows us not only to widely extend and improve our previous results in
[
10
,
11
]
for the speciality
i=2
case, but also to give a thoroughful description (and sometimes classification) of the irreducible components of both the Brill-Noether loci
B_{C}^{k_{2}}(d)\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
and
B_{C}^{k_{3}}(d)\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, in the whole range of interest
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
, cf. (
1.17
) below. Moreover, our approach will also provide important information about the bundle
\mathcal{F}
representing a general point in an irreducible component of a Brill-Noether locus, as well as explicit parametric representations (and so also information on the
birational geometry
) of the constructed components of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, for
i=2,3
.

More precisely, we construct stable bundles
\mathcal{F}
in Brill-Noether loci as extensions
(*)
via suitable line bundles
L
and
N
; Brill–Noether loci we hit in this way therefore depend on the cohomology of the chosen line bundles
L
and
N
, on their extension space
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
and also on the behavior of the
coboundary map

H^{0}(C,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(C,N)
naturally associated to
(*)
(cf. §
1.4
). We exhibit explicit constructions of such bundles when
C
is a general
\nu
-gonal curve, providing existence of (and sometimes even complete classification of all the) irreducible components of the Brill-Noether locus
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
,
i=2,3
and, at the same time, we get
non–emptiness
results for fibres of the
determinant map

B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)\to{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
, sometimes even when such non-emptiness is unexpected by expected dimension (cf. e.g. Corollaries
0.4
and
0.6
below).

To do this, we will let indeed the line bundles
L
and
N
as in
(*)
vary in their own rank-one Brill-Noether loci; accordingly, we will let the constructed bundles
\mathcal{F}
vary in suitable degeneracy loci
\mathcal{W}\subseteq{\rm Ext}^{1}(L,N)
, defined in such a way that
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{W}
general has the desired speciality
i=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})
. We will thus obtain irreducible varieties parametrizing triples
(L,N,\mathcal{F})
and, once the general bundle
\mathcal{F}
is shown to be stable, such varieties will be therefore endowed with a (rational) modular map
\pi
to
U_{C}(d)
, whose image will be contained in a component
\mathcal{B}
of a suitable Brill-Noether locus. To find out a
minimal presentation

(*)
for a general member
\mathcal{F}
in
{\rm im}(\pi)\subseteq\mathcal{B}
will sometimes turn out to be equivalent to finding conditions on line bundles
L
,
N
and on the associated coboundary map
\partial
ensuring the aforementioned map
\pi
to be dominant onto the component
\mathcal{B}
with suitable dimension for its general fiber.

Precisely, in the speciality
i=2
case we prove the following results.

Theorem 0.2
.

(Case
i=2
) Let
g\geq 4
,
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Then, for
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, one has the following cases:

(i)

for
4g-6\leq d\leq 4g-4
,
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)=\emptyset
;

(ii)

for
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
,
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is irreducible, consisting of only one irreducible component, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
, which is regular and uniruled. Moreover, if
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
denotes a general point then the corresponding bundle
\mathcal{F}
is stable, with speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=2
and it arises as an extension of the following type:

(ii-1)
for any
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
, when
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{2}
, or for any

3g-4\leq d\leq 4g-7
, when
\frac{g+1}{2}\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
,
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form:

0\to\omega_{C}(-D)\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0,

where

D\in C^{(4g-5-d)}
and
p\in C
are general;

(ii-2)
for any
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 3g-5
, when
\frac{g+1}{2}\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
,
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form:

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0,

where
p\in C
general,
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+3}(C)
general, i.e. special and non-effective.

(ii-3)
In any of the above cases,
\omega_{C}(-p)
is a line bundle of minimal degree among all effective and special quotient line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
;

(iii)

for
3g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, which occur for
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-2}{2}
(otherwise we are in case
(ii-2)
),
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is reducible, consisting of two irreducible components,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
.

(iii-1)
The component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
is regular and uniruled. A general element
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
which is stable, of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=2
and fitting in an exact sequence as in case
(ii-1)
above, where
p\in C
and
D\in C^{(4g-5-d)}
are general. Moreover,
\omega_{C}(-p)
is of minimal degree among all effective and special quotient line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
.

(iii-2)
The component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
is generically smooth, of dimension
6g-6-d-2\nu>\rho_{d}^{k_{2}}
, i.e. it is
superabundant. Moreover, it is ruled and its general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
which is stable, of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=2
, fitting in an exact sequence of the form:

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
whereas
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
is general of its degree. Moreover,
\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}
is of minimal degree among all quotient line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
.

(iv)

For
2g-4+2\nu\leq d\leq 3g-6
, which occurs for
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-2}{2}
(otherwise
2g-4+2\nu>3g-6
and we are in previous cases),
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is reducible, consisting of two components,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
. The description of
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
is as in
(iii)
whereas description of its general point is identical to that described in case
(iii-2)
above. The description of
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
is as in
(iii)
whereas the description of its general point is identical to that described in case
(ii-2)
.

(v)

For
d=2g-5+2\nu
and for any
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-2}{2}
(otherwise
2g-5+2\nu\geq 3g-4
and we are in previous cases),
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is reducible, consisting of two components,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
. In this case the two components are both regular. The rest of the description is identical to that described in case
(iv)
(except for the superabundance of
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
, which is no-longer true).

(vi)

For
2g-2\leq d\leq 2g-6+2\nu
, for any
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
,
B_{d}^{k_{2}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
is irreducible, consisting of only one irreducible component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
, which is regular and uniruled. The descriptions of such a component and of its general point are identical to those described in case
(iv)
above.

Proof.

The proof is given in full details in Sect.
3
; more precisely it will be given by the collection of Lemma
2.1
, Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.5
,
3.9
,
3.13
,
3.15
,
3.20
, Corollaries
3.12
,
3.24
and Remark
3.25
.
∎

Remark 0.3
.

(i) Notice that Theorem
0.2
strongly improves
[
10
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
11
, Theorem 1.5]
. Indeed
for the whole range of interest
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
, as from (
1.17
), Theorem
0.2
classifies all the irreducible components of
B_{d}^{k_{2}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
and also precisely describes the geometric behavior of all such components, giving in particular important information on their birational structure as well as on the explicit presentation of the general point of any such a component.

(ii) Theorem
0.2
also corrects
[
11
, Theorem 3.12-(ii)]
where, for
d=3g-4,\,3g-5
, it was wrongly stated that the component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
is non-reduced (cf. also Remark
3.14
). This was a consequence of tricky computations on kernels of suitable Petri/multiplication maps. Correct computations are contained in the proof of Proposition
3.15
below, which focuses on a wider range of new cases (cf. also Remark
3.19
).

(iii) Theorem
0.2
exhibits also the irreducibility of
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
as postulated in Theorem
0.1
-(i). Indeed, apart from
4g-6\leq d\leq 4g-4
where
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)=\emptyset
, it turns out that
B^{k_{2}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
is irreducible and regular, for any
2g-2\leq d\leq 2g-6+2\nu
and
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
. These are exactly the ranges of
d
for which no integer
n
(as required in Theorem
0.1
-(i) guaranteeing reducibility) can exist; cf. the proof of Proposition
3.3
and see Remark
3.25
for explicit computations.

(iv) Finally, Theorem
0.2
also shows that Teixidor I Bigas’ component
\mathcal{B}
as in Theorem
0.1
-(i) (existing on any smooth, irreducible projective curve
C
) coincides with our component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
(cf. §
3.1
), for which we moreover prove its
regularity
and
uniruledness
when
C
is a general
\nu
-gonal curve (but the construction still holds for any smooth
C
). We furthermore deduce suitable
minimal presentation
as line-bundle extension for the bundle corresponding to a general point of such a component.

As a direct consequence of Theorem
0.2
one has also further information on some other
Brill-Noether loci
, e.g. those parametrizing (isomorphism classes of) stable vector bundles with fixed determinant, instead of fixed degree. Indeed, in general one has the so called
determinantal map
, simply defined as:

U_{C}(d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\det}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Pic}^{d}(C),\;\;\;[\mathcal{F}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle det}}{{\longrightarrow}}\det(\mathcal{F}),

which turns out to be surjective. For any
given
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
one can therefore consider

U_{C}(M):=(\det)^{-1}(M)=\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)\,|\,\det(\mathcal{F})=M\},

which is well-known to be irreducible and of dimension
3g-3
, as well as for any
i\geq 1
the
Brill-Noether loci with fixed determinant
namely

B_{M}^{k_{i}}:=B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U_{C}(M),

whose
expected dimension
is
\rho_{M}^{k_{2}}:=3g-3-i\,k_{i}
, cf.
[
28
]
. Therefore, from Theorem
0.2
, one has:

Corollary 0.4
.

With assumptions as in Theorem
0.2
and in the above set-up, one has the following situation.

\bullet
For any
2g-5+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{2}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is not empty even when its expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{2}}=7g-11-2d
is negative which, under condition
d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
, occurs for
d>\frac{7g-11}{2}
and
\nu<\frac{g+1}{4}
.

\bullet
Furthermore, for
d\geq 3g-3
, the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{2}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
consists only of
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
, which is superabundant, of dimension
5g-6-d-2\nu
, where
g-1\leq 5g-6-d-2\nu\leq 3g-1-4\nu
, and it is birational to the projective space
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N))
, where
M=K_{C}-A+N\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general as above.

\bullet
For
2g-5+2\nu\leq d\leq{3g-4}
, we have the extra component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
since, in such a case,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
dominates
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
via the determinantal map, as
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2})\geq g=\operatorname{dim}({\rm Pic}^{d}(C))
and
\det(\mathcal{F})=K_{C}-p+N=M
general, as
N
is. Such a component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is of the expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{2}}=7g-11-2d
.

\bullet
When
d=2g-5+2\nu
, also the component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
has expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{2}}=7g-11-2d
.

\bullet
At last, for
2g-2\leq d\leq 2g-6+2\nu
,
B_{M}^{k_{2}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is irreducible and of expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{2}}=7g-11-2d
.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of that in
[
11
, Cor. 3.2, Rem. 3.3]
, for the cases studied therein. For this reason, we will be brief. One simply takes
\mathcal{B}
to be any of the two components of
B_{d}^{k_{2}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
in Theorem
0.2
, namely either
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
or
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
, and studies the determinantal map restricted to
\mathcal{B}
.

For example, in the first case we take
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
general as in Theorem
0.2
; since the corresponding bundle
\mathcal{F}
arises as an element of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
, with
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general, one has that
\det(\mathcal{F})\cong K_{C}-A+N
is general in
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
. Therefore, the restricted determinantal map
det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}}}
is dominant onto
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
. Thus, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general,
B_{M}^{k_{2}}(C)
contains
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}\cap U_{C}(2,M)
. Notice further that
\operatorname{dim}\;(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}\cap U_{C}(2,M))=5g-6-2\nu-d>0
, since
M=K_{C}-A+N\simeq M^{\prime}:=K_{C}-A+N^{\prime}
if and only if
N\simeq N^{\prime}
; therefore from the construction of
B_{\rm sup,2}
conducted in Sect.
3.2
it will be clear that
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}\cap U^{s}_{C}(M)
is birational to
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N))
, where
M\simeq K_{C}-A+N\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
is general.

One can apply the same reasoning also to the other component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2}
as in Theorem
0.2
. However, differently from
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,2}
, when
d\geq 3g-3
such a component cannot dominate
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
as
\operatorname{dim}\;(\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,2})=8g-2d-11<g=\operatorname{dim}\;{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
.
∎

Concerning speciality
i=3
, the situation is much more involved.

According to some general results on special bundles on curves (cf. Theorem
1.14
below), one can approach
parametric constructions
of irreducible components
\mathcal{B}
of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
via extensions as in
(*)
by using descriptions of line bundles in relevant rank-one Brill-Noether loci on a general
\nu
-gonal curve (cf. Lemmas
3.22
and
4.2
) which occur as either quotients or kernels of presentations as in
(*)
. This limits the number of cases to deal with, according to the speciality of the quotient line bundle
L
in the presentation, and it also gives rise to natural descriptions of candidate bundles among
first type
and
second type
(cf. Definition
1.15
below). As a by-product of this strategy, one can immediately find some obstructions for vector bundles to fill-up a possible irreducible component
\mathcal{B}
of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
(cf. e.g. Propositions
4.8
,
4.9
,
4.13
,
4.32
). For example, for
regular
components, one has easy obstructions from the degree itself; namely for
d\geqslant\frac{10}{3}g-5
no
regular
component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
can actually exists since, in this case, one has
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d<0
(cf. Remark
4.7
). When otherwise no direct obstructions are given, one is therefore involved in distinguishing among possible irreducible components of
first type
, of
second type
and also of
modified type
, according to the presentation of the bundle
\mathcal{F}
which is candidate to be a possible general point of a component (cf. Lemma
4.3
and Definition
4.4
below), as well as to perform explicit constructions and to study global and local geometry of the constructed components.

In this set-up, we prove the following:

Theorem 0.5
.

(Case
i=3
) Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Then, for
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, one has the following cases:

(A)

For
g\geq 4
,
3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor
and
4g-6\leq d\leq 4g-4
, one has
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)=\emptyset
.

(B)

For
g\geq 15
,
3\leq\nu<\frac{g-2}{4}
and
4g-4-2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-7
, one has
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)=\emptyset
.

(C)

Concerning irreducible components of first type (in the sense of Definition
4.4
below), we have the following situation:

(C-i) If either
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
4g-4-4\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
or
\frac{g}{6}<\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
4g-4-4\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, there is no irreducible component
\mathcal{B}
of first type of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
.

(C-ii) If
g\geq 18
, then for any integers
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{6}
and
\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
of first type, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}\right)=6g-6-4\nu-d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is of first type as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
4.3
, fitting in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)

is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
, whereas
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
is general. Furthermore,
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}
described above is the only irreducible component whose general point arises from a bundle
\mathcal{F}
of first type on
C
.

(C-iii) If
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq{\rm min}\left\{4g-5-4\nu,\frac{10}{3}g-6\right\}
(more precisely, when
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{6}
one has
\frac{10}{3}g-6\leq 4g-5-4\nu
so we consider
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, when otherwise
\frac{g}{6}<\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
we have
4g-5-4\nu<\frac{10}{3}g-6
, so we consider
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
), there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(B_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)})=6g-6-4\nu-d.

In particular
it is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, when
d\geq 2g-5+2\nu
whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,\,3,\,(1-a)}
, when otherwise
d=2g-6+2\nu
.

Furthermore, its general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in B_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
, speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is of first type as in case
(1-a)
of Lemma
4.3
and fitting in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
, wheras
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
is general and
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(1-a)}
is the only irreducible component whose general point arises from a bundle
\mathcal{F}
of first type on
C
.

(C-iv) If
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
and
2g-2\leq d\leq 2g-7+2\nu
, there is no irreducible component
\mathcal{B}
of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
of first type.

(D)

Concerning irreducible components of modified type (in the sense of Definition
4.4
below), we have the following situation:

(D-i) For any integers
g\geq 5
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+4}{3}
,
3g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and
\nu+1\leq s\leq g-\nu+1
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is obtained by a natural modification of bundles of second type as in case
(2-b)
of Lemma
4.3
. Indeed, such a bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}(-D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general, with
\nu+1\leq s\leq g-\nu+1
as above.

(D-ii) For any integers
g\geq 8
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+4}{4}
,
3g-3-\nu\leq d\leq 3g-6
and
g-\nu+2\leq s\leq g-1
, there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which is obtained by natural modification of bundles of second type as in case
(2-b)
of Lemma
4.3
. Indeed, such a bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}(-D_{s})\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general, where as above
g-\nu+2\leq s\leq g-1
.

(D-iii) For any
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+3}{4}
and
2g-7+3\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, uniruled, generically smooth and superabundant, being of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d>\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,\,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0\,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
whereas
N\in Pic^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
is general of its degree, so non-effective and special.

(D-iv) For any
g\geq 10
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
and
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
there exists an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, which is uniruled, generically smooth and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}\right)=8g-11-2\nu-2d\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}},

whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which fits in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\to 0\,

where
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
whereas
N\in Pic^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
is general of its degree, so non-effective and special. Moreover, the component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, if
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, if
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

(E)

Concerning irreducible components of second type (in the sense of Definition
4.4
below), we have the following situation:

(E-i) For any
g\geqslant 7
,
3\leqslant\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
2g-2\leqslant d\leqslant 3g-7
, the Brill-Noether locus
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
contains a regular component, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
, which is uniruled. For
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
general, the corresponding bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits into an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0,

where
N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+2}(C)
is general (special, non-effective),
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{v}
with
v
general in an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
of the degeneracy locus

\mathcal{W}_{2}:=\{u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)\ |\ {\rm corank}\left(H^{0}(\omega_{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{u}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(N)\right)\geq 2\}\subseteq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)

(cf. (
3.1
) below), where
\partial_{v}
denotes the natural coboundary map, such that the component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is of (expected) codimension
2d-10-4g
(cf. (
3.2
)) and
v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is s.t.
{\rm corank}(\partial_{v})=2
;

Furthermore, the bundle
\mathcal{F}
admits as quotient line bundles also
\omega_{C}(-p)
, for
p\in C
general, so it also fits in the exact sequence

0\to N(p)\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0,

where
p\in C
general and
N(p)\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+3}(C)
general of its degree.

(E-ii) For any
g\geq 9
,
\frac{g+3}{4}\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
and
3g-6\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
, the Brill-Noether locus
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
admits a regular component, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
, whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
corresponds to a rank-
2
stable bundle
\mathcal{F}
of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
, which fits into an exact sequence of the form

0\to\omega_{C}\otimes G_{t}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0,

where
|G_{t}|=g^{2}_{t}
is a base point free and birationally very ample linear system, corresponding to a general point
G_{t}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{t}(C)
, where
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}
the irreducible component
of the line bundle Brill-Noether locus
W^{2}_{t}(C)
as in Lemma
4.2
, where necessarily
\frac{2}{3}g+2\leq t\leq g+2
, and where
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
for
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C},K_{C}-g^{2}_{t})
corresponding to a general point of an irreducible component
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of the degeneracy locus
\mathcal{W}_{2}
similarly as above, such that
\mathcal{U}_{2}
is superabundant, of dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{U}_{2})=2
and such that
{\rm corank}(\partial_{u})=2
, where
\partial_{u}
the natural coboundary map.

Furthermore the bundle
\mathcal{F}
admits as quotient line bundles also
\omega_{C}(-p)
, for
p\in C
general, so it also fits in the exact sequence

0\to K_{C}-g^{2}_{t}+p\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0,

where
p\in C
general.

Proof.

The proof is given in complete details in Sect.
4
.
Precisely, from Lemma
2.1
, we know that for any
2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4
there is no irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]
corresponds to a rank-
2
vector bundle of speciality
j:=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})>3
. Therefore, one can focus on bundles of degree
d
and speciality
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
. The proof of (A) is then given in Proposition
4.1
whereas that of (B) is given in Proposition
4.6
.

For case (C), taking into account Lemma
4.3
and Definition
4.4
below, the uniqueness among components of
first type
is a consequence of Propositions
4.8
,
4.9
-(i),
4.13
and
4.32
; all the rest is proved in Proposition
4.14
.

The proof of (D) is conducted in Proposition
4.22
; whereas the proof of (E) is given in Propositions
4.34
and
4.44
.
∎

Similarly as for the speciality
i=2
case, a direct consequence of Theorem
0.5
is the following:

Corollary 0.6
.

In the assumptions as in Theorem
0.5
, one has the following situation.

\bullet
With numerical assumptions as in (C-ii) and (C-iii) of Theorem
0.5
, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general
the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is not empty, even when its expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d
is negative with
d\leq 4g-5-4\nu
, which occurs for
d>3g-6
and
\nu<\frac{g+1}{4}
.

More precisely, such a locus contains an irreducible component of first type, namely
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a),M}:=U^{s}_{C}(M)\cap\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}
, whose general point corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
as in (C-ii) and (C-iii), with
\det(\mathcal{F})=K_{C}-2A+N=M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general. Moreover
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a),M})=5g-6-4\nu-d
, which is of expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d
if and only if
d=2g-6+2\nu
, otherwise it is superabundant.

\bullet
With numerical assumptions as in (D) of Theorem
0.5
together with
d\leq 3g-6
, then for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is not empty. More precisely, such a locus contains a component of modified type, namely
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}:=U^{s}_{C}(M)\cap\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
,
whose general point corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
as in (D), with
\det(\mathcal{F})=2K_{C}-A-D_{s}=M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, when we are in case (D-i) and (D-ii), whereas
\det(\mathcal{F})=K_{C}-A+N=M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, in cases (D-iii) and (D-iv). Moreover
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=7g-11-2\nu-2d
, which is of expected dimension
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d\geq 0
if and only if
d=2g-7+2\nu
, otherwise it is superabundant.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that in Corollary
0.4
, and it deals with restricting the determinantal map to the irreducible components constructed in Theorem
0.5
. Notice first that, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, from
[
28
]
the
expected dimension
of
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is

\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=3g-3-3k_{3}=9g-18-3d
which is negative if and only if
d>3g-6
.

For the component of
first type
as in Theorem
0.5
-(C-ii) and (C-iii), from the generality of
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
we deduce that
\det(\mathcal{F})=N+K_{C}-2A
is general in
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
. Therefore, the map
\det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}}}
is dominant onto
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
. Hence, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general, one has
\det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}}}^{-1}(M)\neq\emptyset
. Stability and speciality of
\mathcal{F}
as in Theorem
0.5
-(C-ii) and (C-iii) show that
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
is not empty. Moreover, since

M=N+K_{C}-2A\cong M^{\prime}:=N^{\prime}+K_{C}-2A^{\prime}
if and only if
N\cong N^{\prime}
, one deduces the statement about the dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a),M})=5g-6-4\nu-d
. The fact that it is of the
expected dimension

\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d
if and only if
d=2g-6+2\nu
, otherwise it is
superabundant
, follows from the fact that
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}-g
, the dominance of
\det_{|_{\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(1-a)}}}
and the fact that the component of
first type
as in Theorem
0.5
-(C-ii) and (C-iii) is
superabundant
unless
d=2g-6+2\nu
, when it is
regular
.

Similar proof for the case (D) as in Theorem
0.5
, dealing with components of
modified type
. On the other hand, differently from the previous case, notice that for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general the Brill-Noether locus
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
does not contain a component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod,\,M}
from case (D) when
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d<0
. Indeed, for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general,
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
contains a component of modified type only from cases (D-iii) and (D-iv) in Theorem
0.5
. If such a component existed when
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d<0
, namely for
d>3g-6
, then it would come from case (D-iv) and so one should have
3g-6<d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, which would yield
\nu>\frac{g+2}{3}
that cannot occur due to the assumption
\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
in (D-iv).

Finally, components of
second type
as in Theorem
0.5
-(E) do not give rise to irreducible components of
B_{M}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(M)
for
M\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
general; indeed in case (E-i) of Theorem
0.5
we have
d\leq 3g-7
where
\rho_{M}^{k_{3}}=9g-18-3d<0
, whereas in case (E-ii)
\det(\mathcal{F})=2K_{C}-G_{t}
is not general in
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
.
∎

1.
Preliminaries

In this paper we work over
\mathbb{C}
. All schemes will be endowed with the Zariski topology. We will interchangeably use the terms rank-
r
vector bundle on a smooth, projective variety
X
and rank-
r
locally free sheaf; for this reason we may abuse notation and identify divisor classes with the corresponding line bundles, interchangeably using additive and multiplicative notation.
The dual bundle of a rank-
r
vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
; if
L
is of rank-
1
, i.e. it is a line bundle, we therefore interchangeably use
L^{\vee}
or
-L
.

Given a smooth, projective variety
X
, we will denote by
\sim
the linear equivalence of Cartier divisors on
X
and by
\sim_{alg}
their algebraic equivalence. If
\mathcal{M}
is a moduli space, parametrizing objects modulo a given equivalence relation, and if
Y
is a representative of an equivalence class in
\mathcal{M}
, we denote by
[Y]\in\mathcal{M}
the point corresponding to
Y
.

If
C
denotes any smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g
, as customary,
W^{r}_{d}(C)
will denote the
Brill-Noether locus
as in the Introduction, parametrizing line bundles
L\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
such that
h^{0}(L)\geq r+1
. The integer

(1.1)

\rho(g,r,d):=g-(r+1)(g+r-d)

denotes the
Brill-Noether number
and the multiplication map

(1.2)

\mu_{L}:H^{0}(C,L)\otimes H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes L^{\vee})\to H^{0}(C,\omega_{C})

denotes the so called
Petri map
of the line bundle
L
. As for the rest, we will use standard terminology and notation as in e.g.
[
2
]
,
[
20
]
.

1.1.
Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on general
\nu
-gonal curves

In this section we will briefly review some basic results concerning Brill-Noether theory of line bundles on a general
\nu
-gonal curve, which will be used below in the paper. From the Introduction, given a positive integer
g
, from now on we will consider
\nu
an integer s.t.

(1.3)

3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor,\;{\rm in\;particular}\;g\geq 4.

We will say that a smooth, irreducible, projective curve
C
is a

general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
if
[C]
is a general point of the
\nu
-gonal stratum

\mathcal{M}^{1}_{g,\nu}\subsetneq\mathcal{M}_{g}
, where
\mathcal{M}^{1}_{g,\nu}
is obtained as the image via the natural modular map
\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{M}_{g}
as in the Introduction, where
\mathcal{H}_{g,\nu}
the
Hurwitz scheme
mentioned therein. We first remind the following:

Proposition 1.1

(cf.
[
3
]
)
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
, let
r
be a non-negative integer and let
A\in{\rm Pic}^{\nu}(C)
be the unique line bundle on
C
associated to the unique base-point-free pencil on
C
of degree
\nu
, i.e.
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
. If
g\geq r(\nu-1)
, then
\operatorname{dim}(|A^{\otimes r}|)=r
whereas if
g<r(\nu-1)
then
A^{\otimes r}
is non-special hence
\operatorname{dim}(|A^{\otimes r}|)=r\,\nu-g>r
.

Recent papers (cf. e.g.
[
14
,
22
,
23
,
29
,
30
,
36
]
) have addressed the study of Brill-Noether theory on a general

\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
via a new approach, based on the fact that given such a curve and denoted by

C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
the map giving rise to the degree-
\nu
ramified cover over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and taking any

L\in{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
, the splitting types of rank-
\nu
bundles
f_{*}(L)
over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, when
L
varies, capture more precise information concerning Brill-Noether theory on
C
, yielding a better control of
W_{d}^{r}(C)
, giving rise to a refinement of the stratification of
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
by Brill-Noether loci
W^{r}_{d}(C)
’s. Using this approach, the aforementioned authors proved breakthrough results in this topic. For reader’s convenience, below we give a brief overview of results which will be sometimes used in the sequel.

Let therefore
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
, with
f:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
denoting the morphism of degree
\nu
, and
let
L
be a line bundle of degree
d
on
C
; then
f_{*}(L)
is a rank-
\nu
vector bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By Grothendieck theorem, there exist uniquely determined integers
e_{1}\leq e_{2}\leq\ldots\leq e_{\nu}
such that
f_{*}(L)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{\nu})
, with

(1.4)

\mbox{deg}(f_{*}(L))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}e_{i}=d+1-g-\nu,

the latter equality following from Riemann-Roch theorem and Leray’s isomorphism. Moreover, by projection formula, if
h^{0}(C,L)=r+1
, then

(1.5)

r+1=h^{0}(C,L)=h^{0}\left(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{\nu})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}{\rm max}\,\{e_{i}+1,\,0\}.

Following
[
14
,
30
]
, for any collection
(e_{1},\dots,e_{\nu})
of such integers, one sets
\vec{e}:=(e_{1},\dots,e_{\nu})
, which is called the
splitting type vector
of the bundle
f_{*}(L)
, and sets moreover
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}):=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{\nu})
. The splitting type obviously is a more refined invariant than the rank and the degree of the bundle
f_{*}(L)
, as it encodes information about all the bundles
f_{*}\left(L\otimes f^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)\right)\right)
, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
; indeed, by projection formula:

h^{0}\left(C,L\otimes f^{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)\right)\right)=h^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}{\rm max}\{m+e_{i}+1,\,0\},\;\forall\;m\in\mathbb{Z}.

For a given splitting type vector
\vec{e}
, one considers the
Brill-Noether splitting locus

W^{\vec{e}}(C):=\{L\in Pic^{d}(C)\,|\,f_{*}(L)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(e_{\nu})\},

which is locally-closed in
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
(the above notation is more similar to Brill-Noether notation and it is taken from
[
14
,
29
]
, whereas in
[
30
]
the above locus is denoted by
\Sigma_{\vec{e}}(C,f)
). Its
expected codimension
is given by
the
magnitude
of
\vec{e}
(c.f.
[
14
, Def. 2.3]
), which is

(1.6)

u(\vec{e}):=h^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq\nu}{\rm max}\,\{0,\;e_{j}-e_{1}-1\}.

Since
\vec{e}
can be thought as a partition with possibly negative parts, splitting type vectors follow
certain
“specialization” rules
: if
\vec{e}
and
\vec{e}\,^{\prime}
are two splitting type vectors,
one defines a partial ordering
\vec{e}\,^{\prime}\leq\vec{e}
if
e_{1}^{\prime}+e_{2}^{\prime}+\ldots+e_{j}^{\prime}\leq e_{1}+e_{2}+\ldots+e_{j}
, for any

1\leq j\leq\nu
, and this in turn implies that the bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e})

specializes to

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}\,^{\prime})
(cf. e.g.
[
30
, § 2]
). Thus
one can define the
Brill-Noether splitting degeneracy loci

\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}:=\left\{L\in Pic^{d}(C)\,|\,h^{0}(C,L\otimes f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)))\geq h^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\;\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(m)),\;\;\forall\;m\in\mathbb{Z}\right\},

and gets that
\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}=\bigcup_{\vec{e}\,^{\prime}\leq\vec{e}}W^{\vec{e}\,^{\prime}}
, cf.
[
30
, (2.1)]
. A first set of results is given by the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g\geq 4
. Let
\vec{e}
be a splitting type vector as above, satisfying (
1.4
) and let
u(\vec{e})
be its magnitude as in (
1.6
). Consider the integer

(1.7)

\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e}):=g-u(\vec{e}),

which is the expected dimension of
W^{\vec{e}}
. Then:

(i)
W^{\vec{e}}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e})\geq 0
. Moreover, when not empty,

W^{\vec{e}}
is of pure dimension
{\rm min}\;\{g,\,\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e})\}
(cf.
[
14
]
,
[
30
, Thm. 1.2]
);

(ii) When
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e})\geq 0
,
W^{\vec{e}}
is smooth (cf.
[
30
, Thm. 1.2]
). More precisely, when

\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}\subsetneq{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
, then
\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}
is normal, Cohen-Macaulay and it is smooth
away from the union of the splitting loci
W^{\vec{e}\,^{\prime}}
, with
\vec{e}\,^{\prime}\leq\vec{e}
having
codimension more than
2
(cf.
[
29
, Thm. 1.2-(2’)]
);

(iii)
\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}
is irreducible when
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e})>0
. When otherwise

\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e})=0
, the number of points of
\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}
is computed via Coxeter groups
(cf.
[
29
, Thm. 1.2]
);

(iv) When
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{e})\geq 0
, the universal Brill-Noether splitting degeneracy locus
\overline{\mathcal{W}^{\vec{e}}}
has a unique component dominating the Hurwitz scheme
{\mathcal{H}}_{g,\nu}
(cf.
[
29
, Thm. 1.2]
).

Such Brill-Noether splitting degeneracy loci provide a refinement of the stratification of
{\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
by Brill-Noether loci
W_{d}^{r}(C)
; indeed, fixed a rank
r^{\prime}
and a degree
d^{\prime}
for vector bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, there exist a unique
splitting type which is
maximal
w.r.t. the previously defined partial order and this is called the
balanced splitting type
, which is characterized
by the condition
|e_{i}-e_{j}|\leq 1
, for all
1\leq i,j\leq r^{\prime}
. The corresponding
balanced bundle
is denoted by
B(r^{\prime},d^{\prime})
whereas the corresponding splitting type vector is said to be
maximal
(cf.
[
30
, p. 773–774]
). Thus, posing
|\vec{e}|:=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}e_{i}
, one gets (cf.
[
30
, (2.2)]
)

(1.8)

W_{d}^{r}(C)=\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{e}\;{\rm s.t.}\;|\vec{e}|=d-g+1+\nu\\
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))\geq r+1\end{array}}W^{\vec{e}}=\bigcup_{\begin{array}[]{c}\vec{e}\;{\rm maximal\;s.t.}\;|\vec{e}|=d-g+1+\nu\\
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))\geq r+1\end{array}}\overline{W^{\vec{e}}}.

Therefore, characterizing contributions of splitting loci to the Brill-Noether locus
W_{d}^{r}(C)
is equivalent to
determining those splitting type vectors that are maximal, w.r.t. the partial order given above, among those satisfying:

(1.9)

\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}e_{i}=d+1-g-\nu\;\;{\rm and}\;\;h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}{\rm max}\,\{0,\,e_{i}+1\}\geq r+1,

as in (
1.4
) and (
1.5
). These are the so-called

“balanced plus balanced”
splitting type vectors in
[
30
, p. 769]
, which are uniquely determined by the number of their non-negative components. With this said, the following is a second set of results in this direction.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g\geq 4
and let
r
and
d
be non-negative integers.

(1)
If
r\leq d-g
, then
W_{d}^{r}(C)={\rm Pic}^{d}(C)
(Riemann-Roch theorem; cf. also
[
30
, Rem. p. 774]
);

(2)
If
r>d-g
, set
d^{\prime}:=d+1-g-\nu
. Then:

(2-i)

a splitting type vector
\vec{e}
, which is maximal among those satisfying (
1.9
),
satisfies the condition
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{e}))=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}{\rm max}\,\{0,\,e_{i}+1\}=r+1
(cf.
[
14
, Lemma 2.7]
);

(2-ii)

maximal splitting type vectors as in (2-i) are vectors
\vec{w}_{r,\ell}
such that

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=B(\nu-r-1+\ell,\;d^{\prime}-\ell)\oplus B(r+1-\ell,\;\ell),

where
B(-,\;-)

as above denotes the unique balanced bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of given rank and degree, respectively, for

{\rm max}\;\{0,\;r+2-\nu\}\leq\ell\leq r
an integer such that either
\ell=0
or
\ell\leq g+2r+1-d-\nu
. Moreover

u(\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=g-\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=g-\left(\rho(g,r-\ell,d)-\ell\,\nu\right),

where
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{r,\ell})
as in (
1.7
) (cf.
[
30
, Lemma 2.2]
);

(2-iii)

for any integer
\ell
as in (2-ii), the corresponding splitting type vector
\vec{w}_{r,\ell}
is the unique
degree–
d^{\prime}
, rank–
\nu
vector with
r+1-\ell
non-negative coordinates, which is maximal among those with
r+1
linearly independent global sections (cf.
[
30
, Cor. 1.3]
);

(2-iv)

Every component of
W_{d}^{r}(C)
is generically smooth, of (expected) dimension
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=g-u(\vec{w}_{r,\ell})=\rho(g,r-\ell,d)-\ell\,\nu
, for some integer
{\rm max}\;\{0,\;r+2-\nu\}\leq\ell\leq r
such that either
\ell=0
or
\ell\leq g+2r+1-d-\nu
. Such a component exists for each integer
\ell
as above satisfying furthermore
\rho^{\prime}(g,\vec{w}_{r,\ell})\geq 0
(cf.
[
30
, Cor. 1.3]
).

1.2.
Ruled surfaces and unisecants

Let
C
be any smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-two vector bundle on
C
. Then
F:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\to}}C
will denote the
(geometrically) ruled
surface
(or simply the
surface scroll
) associated to the pair
(\mathcal{F},C)
;
f
will denote the general
\rho
-fibre and

\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)
the
tautological line bundle
. If
H
denotes a divisor in
|\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)|
and if
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is another divisor on
F
, set
{\rm deg}(\widetilde{\Gamma}):=\widetilde{\Gamma}H
. In particular we set

d:=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})=\mbox{deg}(\det(\mathcal{F}))=H^{2}=\mbox{deg}(H)
. The integer
i(\mathcal{F}):=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})
is called the
speciality
of
\mathcal{F}
and will be simply denoted by
i
, if there is no danger of confusion.
\mathcal{F}
(and also
F
) is said to be
non-special
if
i=0
,
special
otherwise.

We briefly recall some basic facts on unisecant curves of the surface scroll
F
; for more details the reader is referred to e.g.
[
20
, V-2]
or to
[
12
, § 2]
. Let
{\rm Div}^{1}_{F}
be the subscheme of
{\rm Div}_{F}
formed by all divisors

\widetilde{\Gamma}
of
F
such that
\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\rho^{*}(N^{\vee})
, for some
N\in{\rm Pic}(C)
. One has a natural morphism

\Psi_{1}:{\rm Div}^{1}_{F}\to{\rm Pic}(C)
, defined by
\widetilde{\Gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Psi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}N
. If
D\in{\rm Div}(C)
, then
\rho^{*}(D)
will be denoted by
f_{D}
, so
\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}^{1}_{F}

if and only if
\widetilde{\Gamma}\sim H-f_{D}
, for some
D\in{\rm Div}(C)
, and
\mbox{deg}(\widetilde{\Gamma})=\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F})-\mbox{deg}(D)
. Curves in
{\rm Div}^{1}_{F}
are called
unisecants
of the surface scroll
F
and irreducible unisecants are isomorphic to
C
and called
sections
of
F
. For any positive integer
\delta
, we consider

{\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}:=\{\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}_{F}^{1}\;|\;{\rm deg}(\widetilde{\Gamma})=\delta\}
, the
Hilbert scheme of unisecants of
F
of degree
\delta
(w.r.t.
H
).

Let
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma+f_{A}
be a unisecant as above, with
\Gamma
a section of
F
and
A
an effective divisor of
C
. Then we have the exact sequence

(1.10)

0\to N(-A)\to\mathcal{F}\to L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{A}\to 0;

in particular if
A=0
, i.e.

\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma
is a section of
F
, then
\mathcal{F}
fits in

(1.11)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to L\to 0

and the normal bundle of
\Gamma
in
F
is:

(1.12)

\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma/F}\simeq L\otimes N^{\vee},\;\;{\rm so}\;\;\Gamma^{2}=\mbox{deg}(L)-\mbox{deg}(N)=2\delta-d

(cf. e.g.
[
12
, Rem. 2.1]
). Accordingly
\Psi_{1,\delta}:{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}\to{\rm Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
, which denotes the restriction of the map
\Psi_{1}
to
{\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}
,
endows
{\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}
with the structure of a Quot scheme, namely as in
[
41
, § 4.4]
, one has

(1.13)

\begin{array}[]{rrcc}\Psi_{1,\delta}:&{\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}&{\rm Quot}^{C}_{\mathcal{F},\delta+t-g+1}\\
&\widetilde{\Gamma}&\longrightarrow&\left\{\mathcal{F}\to\!\!\to L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{A}\right\}.\end{array}

From standard results (cf. e.g.
[
41
, § 4.4]
), (
1.13
) gives
identifications between tangent and obstruction spaces, i.e. using (
1.10
) we have

H^{0}(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}/F})\simeq T_{[\widetilde{\Gamma}]}({\rm Div}_{F}^{1,\delta})\simeq{\rm Hom}(N(-A),L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{A})
and
H^{1}(\mathcal{N}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}/F})\simeq{\rm Ext}^{1}(N(-A),L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{A})
. Finally, if
\widetilde{\Gamma}\sim H-f_{D}
on
F
for some effective divisor
D
on
C
, then one has

(1.14)

|\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|\simeq\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\mathcal{F}(-D))).

Definition 1.4
.

A unisecant
\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
is said to be:

(a) linearly isolated (li) if
\operatorname{dim}(|\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|)=0
,

(b) algebraically isolated (ai) if
\operatorname{dim}({\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F})=0
.

In this paper we will be concerned with speciality of unisecants of a (necessarily special) surface scroll
F
. For this reason, we recall the following terminology.

Definition 1.5
.

(cf.
[
12
, Def. 2.7]
) For
\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
, we set
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}(1):=\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
. The speciality
of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is
i(\widetilde{\Gamma}):=h^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}(1))
.
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is said to be special if
i(\widetilde{\Gamma})>0
.

If
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is given by (
1.10
), then by (
1.14
) one has

\widetilde{\Gamma}\in|\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\rho^{*}(N^{\vee}(A))|
. Applying
\rho_{*}
to the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{F}(-\widetilde{\Gamma})\to\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\to\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}(1)\to 0
and using

\rho_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{F}(1)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{F}(-\widetilde{\Gamma}))\simeq N(-A)
and
R^{1}\rho_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{F}(\rho^{*}(N(-A)))=0
, we get

(1.15)

i(\widetilde{\Gamma})=h^{1}(L\oplus\mathcal{O}_{A})=h^{1}(L)=i(\Gamma),

where
\Gamma
is the unique section contained in
\widetilde{\Gamma}
.

In general, speciality is not constant either in linear systems or in algebraic families of unisecants (cf. e.g.
[
12
, Examples 2.8, 2.9]
). Nonetheless, since
{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
has a structure of Quot-scheme, we can consider some other ingredients in order to study how speciality varies in families. In fact, by the structure of Quot-scheme, there is the universal quotient
\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta}\to{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
so, taking
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta}):={\rm Proj}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
, one can consider

(1.16)

\mathcal{S}_{F}^{1,\delta}:=\{\widetilde{\Gamma}\in{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}\;\;|\;\;R^{1}p_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta})}(1))_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\neq 0\}\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;a_{F}(\delta):=\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{S}_{F}^{1,\delta}),

i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{F}^{1,\delta}
is the support of
R^{1}p_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}_{1,\delta})}(1))
and it parametrizes degree
\delta
, special unisecants of
F
.

Definition 1.6
.

(cf.
[
12
, Def. 2.10]
) Let
\widetilde{\Gamma}
be a special unisecant of the scroll
F
.
Assume
\widetilde{\Gamma}\in\mathfrak{F}
, where
\mathfrak{F}\subseteq{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
is an irreducible subscheme.

(1)
We will say that
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is:

(1-i) specially unique (su) in
\mathfrak{F}
, if
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is the only special unisecant in
\mathfrak{F}
, or

(1-ii) specially isolated (si) in
\mathfrak{F}
, if
\operatorname{dim}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\left(\mathcal{S}_{F}^{1,\delta}\cap\mathfrak{F}\right)=0
.

(2)
In particular:

(2-i) when
\mathfrak{F}=|\mathcal{O}_{F}(\widetilde{\Gamma})|
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is said to be linearly specially unique (lsu) in case (1-i) and linearly specially isolated (lsi) in case (1-ii);

(2-ii) when
\mathfrak{F}={\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is said to be algebraically specially unique (asu) in case (1-i) and
algebraically specially isolated (asi) in case (1-ii).

(3)
When a section
\Gamma\subset F
is asi,
\mathcal{F}
is said to be rigidly specially presented (rsp) by the sequence (
1.11
) corresponding to
\Gamma
. When
\Gamma
is ai (cf. Def.
1.4
), we will say that

\mathcal{F}
is rigidly presented (rp) via (
1.11
).

We conclude by recalling the following results, which will be sometimes used in the sequel.

Proposition 1.7
.

(cf.
[
12
, Lemma 2.11, Prop. 2.12]
) (1) Let
\Gamma\subset F
be a section corresponding to the exact sequence (
1.11
). A section
\Gamma^{\prime}
, corresponding to a quotient
\mathcal{F}\to\!\!\!\!\!\to L^{\prime}
, is s.t.

\Gamma\sim\Gamma^{\prime}
on
F
if and only if
L\simeq L^{\prime}
. In particular
i(\Gamma)=i(\Gamma^{\prime})
; moreover

\Gamma
is lsu if and only if it is lsi if and only if it is li.

(2) Assume
F
to be indecomposable, i.e.
\mathcal{F}
non-splitting, and let
\Gamma\in\mathfrak{F}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{1,\delta}_{F}
be a section, where
\mathfrak{F}
is an irreducible, projective scheme of dimension
k
, parametrizing a flat family of unisecants. Assume that:

(2-i)
k\geq 1
, if
\mathfrak{F}
is a linear system;

(2-ii) either
k\geq 2
, or
k=1
and
\mathfrak{F}
with base points, if
\mathfrak{F}
is not a linear family.

Then,
\mathfrak{F}
contains reducible unisecants
\widetilde{\Gamma}
with
i(\widetilde{\Gamma})\geq i(\Gamma)
.

1.3.
Moduli spaces of rank-two (semi)stable bundles and Brill Nother loci

Given a
rank-two vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
on a smooth, irreducible projective curve
C
of genus
g
, recall that
\mathcal{F}
is said to be
stable
(resp.
semistable
) if for every sub-line bundle
N\subset\mathcal{F}
one has
\mu(N)<\mu(\mathcal{F})
(resp.
\mu(N)\leq\mu(\mathcal{F})
), where

\mu(\mathcal{E}):=\frac{\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{E})}{{\rm rk}(\mathcal{E})}
is the
slope
of any vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
C
. As in the Introduction,
U_{C}(d)
will denote the moduli space of (semi)stable, degree
d
, rank-two vector bundles on
C
, which is irreducible of dimension
4g-3
.
The subset
U_{C}^{s}(d)\subseteq U_{C}(d)
parametrizing (isomorphism classes of) stable bundles, is an open, dense subset. The points in
{U_{C}}^{ss}(d):=U_{C}(d)\setminus U_{C}^{s}(d)
correspond to (S-equivalence classes of)
strictly semistable
bundles (cf. e.g.
[
37
,
40
]
).

Proposition 1.8
.

(cf. e.g.
[
12
, Prop. 3.1]
) Let
C
be a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 1
and let
d
be an integer.

(i) If
d\geq 4g-3
, then for any
[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)
, one has
i(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

(ii) If
g\geq 2
and
d\geq 2g-2
, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)
general, one has
i(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=0
.

From Proposition
1.8
, Serre duality and invariance of stability under operations like tensoring with a line bundle or passing to the dual bundle, for
g\geq 2
it therefore makes sense to consider the proper sub-loci of

U_{C}(d)
parametrizing classes
[\mathcal{F}]
such that
i(\mathcal{F})>0
in the following range for
d
:

(1.17)

2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4.

Definition 1.9
.

Given non-negative integers
d
,
g
and
i
, we set

(1.18)

k_{i}:=d-2g+2+i.

Given a smooth, irreducible, projective curve
C
of genus
g\geq 2
, we define

(1.19)

B^{k_{i}}_{d}:=\left\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)\,|\,h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\geq k_{i}\right\}=\left\{[\mathcal{F}]\in U_{C}(d)\,|\,h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=i(\mathcal{F})\geq i\right\},

which we call the
k_{i}^{th}
–Brill-Noether locus,
simply Brill-Noether locus, when no confusion arises.

Remark 1.10
.

Brill-Noether loci
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
have a natural structure of (locally) determinantal subschemes of
U_{C}(d)
:

(a)
When
d
is odd,
U_{C}(d)=U^{s}_{C}(d)
, then
U_{C}(d)
is a fine moduli space and the existence of a universal bundle on
C\times U_{C}(d)
allows one to construct
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
as the degeneracy locus of a morphism between suitable vector bundles on
U_{C}(d)
(see, e.g.
[
19
]
for details). Accordingly, the
expected dimension
of
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
is

{\rm max}\{-1,\ \rho_{d}^{k_{i}}\}
, where

(1.20)

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}:=4g-3-ik_{i}

is the
Brill-Noether number
in rank-two. If
\emptyset\neq B^{k_{i}}_{d}\subsetneq U_{C}(d)
,
then
B^{k_{i}+1}_{d}\subseteq{\rm Sing}(B^{k_{i}}_{d})
. Since any
[\mathcal{F}]\in B^{k_{i}}_{d}
is stable, it is a smooth point of
U_{C}(d)
and the Zariski tangent space
T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(U_{C}(d))
at the point
[\mathcal{F}]
to
U_{C}(d)
can be identified with
H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})^{\vee}
. If
[\mathcal{F}]\in B^{k_{i}}_{d}\setminus B^{k_{i}+1}_{d}
,
the tangent space to
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
at
[\mathcal{F}]
is the annihilator of the image of the cup–product, i.e. of the
Petri map
of
\mathcal{F}
(see, e.g.
[
44
]
)

(1.21)

\mu_{\mathcal{F}}:H^{0}(C,\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\longrightarrow H^{0}(C,\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}).

If
[\mathcal{F}]\in B^{k_{i}}_{d}\setminus B^{k_{i}+1}_{d}
, then

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}=h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})-h^{0}(C,\mathcal{F})h^{1}(C,\mathcal{F})
and
B^{k_{i}}_{d}
is non–singular, of the expected dimension at
[\mathcal{F}]
if and only if the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is injective.

(b)
When
d
is even,
U_{C}(d)
is not a fine moduli space (because
U^{ss}_{C}(d)\neq\emptyset
). There is a suitable open, non-empty subscheme
\mathcal{Q}^{ss}\subset\mathcal{Q}
of a certain Quot scheme
\mathcal{Q}
defining
U_{C}(d)
via a GIT-quotient map
\pi
(cf. e.g.
[
45
]
for details) and one can define
B^{k_{i}}_{C}(d)
as the image via
\pi
of the degeneracy locus of a morphism between suitable vector bundles on
\mathcal{Q}^{ss}
.

It may happen for a component
\mathcal{B}
of a Brill–Noether locus to be totally contained in
U_{C}^{ss}(d)
;
in this case the lower bound
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
for the expected dimension of
\mathcal{B}
is no longer valid (cf. e.g.

[
9
, Remark 7.4]
). Nonetheless, the lower bound
\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
is still valid if
\mathcal{B}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)\neq\emptyset
.

Recall, finally, the following terminology.

Definition 1.11
.

Assume
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\neq\emptyset
. A component
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{i}}

such that
\mathcal{B}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)\neq\emptyset
will be called regular, if it is generically smooth and of
(expected) dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})=\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
,
superabundant, otherwise.

1.4.
Segre invariant and extensions of line bundles

In what follows,
C
will always denote a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 3
. Given a rank-two vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
on
C
, its
Segre invariant

s(\mathcal{F})\in\mathbb{Z}
is defined to be

s(\mathcal{F})=\min_{N\subset\mathcal{F}}\left\{\mbox{deg}\;\mathcal{F}-2\;\mbox{deg}\;N\right\},

where
N
runs through all the sub-line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
. One has

s(\mathcal{F})=s(\mathcal{F}\otimes L)
, for any line bundle
L
, and

s(\mathcal{F})=s(\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
. A sub-line bundle
N\subset\mathcal{F}
is called a
maximal degree sub-line bundle
of
\mathcal{F}
if
\mbox{deg}(N)
is maximal among all sub-line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
; in such a case
\mathcal{F}/N
is a
minimal degree quotient line bundle
of
\mathcal{F}
, i.e. it is of minimal degree among quotient line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
.

In particular, if
\Gamma\in{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F}
is a section s.t.
\Gamma^{2}=s(\mathcal{F})
, then

s(\mathcal{F})=2\delta-d
by (
1.12
) and
\Gamma
is said to be a section of
minimal degree
(equivalently, of
minimal self-intersection
) on the surface scroll
F=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
, i.e. for any other section
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset F
one has
\mbox{deg}(\Gamma^{\prime})\geq\mbox{deg}(\Gamma)
w.r.t. the tautological line bundle
H
. Thus, from §
1.3
, the bundle
\mathcal{F}
is
semistable
(resp.
stable
) if and only if

s(\mathcal{F})\geq 0
(resp.
s(\mathcal{F})>0
).

Let now
\delta
be a positive integer. Consider
L\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{\delta}(C)
and
N\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
.
The extension space
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
parametrizes (strong) isomorphism classes of extensions and any vector
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
gives rise to a degree
d
, rank-two vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
, fitting in an exact sequence

(1.22)

(u):\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to L\to 0

(which actually coincides with sequence
(*)
as in the Introduction). If
\Gamma
is the section on the surface scroll
F_{u}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
corresponding to the quotient line bundle
L
of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
, since
\Gamma^{2}=\mbox{deg}(L-N)
, in order for
\mathcal{F}_{u}
to be semistable, a necessary condition is therefore

(1.23)

\Gamma^{2}=2\delta-d\geq s(\mathcal{F}_{u})\geq 0.

In such a case, the Riemann-Roch theorem gives

(1.24)

\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=\begin{cases}\ 2\delta-d+g-1\ &\text{ if }L\ncong N\\
\ g\ &\text{ if }L\simeq N.\end{cases}

Lemma 1.12
.

(cf.
[
12
, Lemma 4.1]
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a (semi)stable, special, rank-two vector bundle on
C
of degree
d\geq 2g-2
. Then
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
, for a special and effective line bundle
L\in{\rm Pic}^{\delta}(C)
, for some
\delta\geq\frac{d}{2}
,
N=\det(\mathcal{F})\otimes L^{\vee}\in{\rm Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
and a vector
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(L,N)
as in (
1.22
).

Remark 1.13
.

With assumptions as in Lemma
1.12
, it turns out that any (semi)stable,
rank-two, special vector bundle of degree
d
must be
“presented”
as an extension (
1.22
), where the quotient
line bundle
L
must be effective, with speciality
1\leq h^{1}(C,L)\leq i(\mathcal{F})
, of degree
\delta\geq\frac{d}{2}
(strict inequality when stability holds). In terms of the surface scroll
F=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
, if we let
\Gamma\subset F
be the section corresponding to such a line bundle
L
, and if we moreover assume that it is of minimal degree among quotient line bundles of
\mathcal{F}
then, from Proposition
1.7
-(2), if
\mathcal{F}
is indecomposable (as it occurs if e.g.
\mathcal{F}
is not polystable),
\Gamma\subset F
is (li) with
a_{F}(\delta)\leqslant 1
, where
a_{F}(\delta)
as in (
1.16
); moreover,
\mathcal{F}
is (rsp) via the quotient line bundle
L
if
a_{F}(\delta)=0
, and it is even (rp) if
\Gamma
is (ai) on
F
.

From Lemma
1.12
, for every (semi)stable, special, rank-two vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
on
C
of degree
d\geq 2g-2
, there exist a special and effective line bundle
L
on
C
such that, considered
N:=\det(\mathcal{F})\otimes L^{\vee}
, there exists
u\in Ext^{1}(L,N)
for which
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
. In this set-up, we recall the following:

Theorem 1.14
.

(cf.
[
5
, Prop. 1]
) Let
C
be a smooth, irreducible, projective curve of genus
g\geq 3
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle on
C
of degree
d\geq 2g-2
, with speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})\geq 2
. Then
\mathcal{F}
fits in an exact sequence of the form

(1.25)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-D)\to 0,

where
D
is an effective divisor on
C
such that either
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=1
or
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=i
.

Proof.

This is the “Serre-dual” version of
[
5
, Lemma 1]
or even of
[
44
, Cor. 1.2]
, cited therein
as a direct consequence on an unpublished result of B. Feinberg. This result has been completely proved in
[
5
, Prop. 1]
.
∎

Definition 1.15
.

A bundle
\mathcal{F}
fitting in (
1.25
) and which satisfies
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=i
is said to be a bundle of first type; otherwise if
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=1
then
\mathcal{F}
is said to be a bundle of second type (cf.
[
5
, Def. 1]
).

In the following sections, we are going to construct (and sometimes also classify all the) irreducible components
\mathcal{B}
of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, for
i=2,3
, with the use of the above subdivision in presentations of the rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
corresponding to a general point
[\mathcal{F}]
of such a component
\mathcal{B}
, namely a bundle
\mathcal{F}
which is either of
first type
or of
second type
in the sense of Definition
1.15
, or even suitable natural
modifications
of these two types of presentations, when such a variation let the construction of such a component
\mathcal{B}
to be easier (cf. e.g. Proposition
4.22
below).

We conclude this section by recalling a result by Lange and Narasimhan, cf.
[
27
]
, which allows (via a beautiful geometric approach) to deduce when a bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
as in (
1.22
) turns out to be (semi)stable. When
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
corresponds to a non-trivial sequence (
1.22
), it therefore defines a point
[u]\in\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))\simeq\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(K_{C}+L-N)^{\vee}):=\mathbb{P}
. When the natural map
\varphi:=\varphi_{|K_{C}+L-N|}:C\to\mathbb{P}
is a morphism, set
X:=\varphi(C)\subset\mathbb{P}
and consider, for any positive integer
h
, the
h^{th}
-
secant variety
of
X
, denoted by
\mathrm{Sec}_{h}(X)\subseteq\mathbb{P}
and defined as the closure of the union of all linear subspaces
\langle\varphi(D)\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{P}
,
for general effective divisors
D
of degree
h
on
C
for which
\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Sec}_{h}(X))=\min\,\{\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P})\;,\;2h-1\}
. In this set-up, one has the following:

Proposition 1.16
.

(
[
27
, Proposition 1.1]
) Assume
2\delta-d\geq 2
. Then
\varphi
is a morphism and, for any integer

\sigma\equiv 2\delta-d\ \text{ (mod\ 2) }
such that
4+d-2\delta\leq\sigma\leq 2\delta-d
, one has that
s(\mathcal{F}_{u})\geq\sigma
iff
[u]\notin\mathrm{Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d+\sigma-2)}(X)
.

2.
Brill-Noether loci in speciality
i=2,\;3
: a preliminary result

In this section we give some brief preliminaries to prove Theorems
0.2
and
0.5
. Therefore from now on, unless otherwise stated, we will consider
C
to be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g\geq 4
and, as in (
1.3
) and (
1.17
), we will set

3\leq\nu<\lfloor\frac{g+3}{2}\rfloor\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;2g-2\leq d\leq 4g-4.

Given
i>0
an integer, we set
k_{i}=d-2g+2+i
as in (
1.18
) and, correspondingly, we denote by
B_{d}^{k_{i}}
the Brill-Noether locus as in (
1.19
), whose expected dimension is

\rho_{d}^{k_{i}}
as in (
1.20
).

To prove Theorems
0.2
and
0.5
we will make use of Lemma
1.12
and of Theorem
1.14
from
which we know that, for any possible irreducible component
\mathcal{B}
of
B_{d}^{k_{i}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, its general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
corresponds
to a rank-
2
, degree
d
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
arising as an extension (
1.22
), equivalently (
1.25
), with quotient line bundle
L=\omega_{C}(-D)
being effective, special, of bounded degree
\mbox{deg}(L):=\delta=2g-2-\mbox{deg}(D)>\frac{d}{2}
from stability condition
(
1.23
). The next preliminary result restricts possibilities for the speciality of the quotient line bundle
