and now we will prove that
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=d-2g+7-2\nu
. Consider the exact sequence (
4.31
), where
D_{s}\in C^{(s)}
is general, and tensor it by the line bundle
A
, which gives

(4.32)

0\to K_{C}-D_{s}+A\to\mathcal{F}\otimes A\to K_{C}\to 0.

Notice that
h^{1}(K_{C}-D_{s}+A)=h^{0}(D_{s}-A)=0
, thus
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=h^{1}(K_{C})=1
. The first row of the above diagram completes to the long exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F},\,A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(A)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)\to 0.

Since
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
,
h^{0}(A)=2
and
h^{1}(F\otimes A)=1
, one gets therefore
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\geq 5
. We want to prove that equality holds. If we tensor (
4.31
) by
A^{\vee}
we get

(4.33)

0\to K_{C}-D_{s}-A\to\mathcal{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0.

Since
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\geq 5
and
h^{1}(K_{C}-2A)=h^{0}(2A)=3
(cf. Proposition
1.1
), the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{v}
corresponds to an element

v\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}=\left\{w\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,K_{C}-D_{s}-A)\ |\ \operatorname{corank}(\partial_{w})\geq 2\right\}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,K_{C}-D_{s}-A).

By the description of such a degeneracy locus as in (
3.1
), it follows that
v
corresponds to a hyperplane in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,K_{C}-D_{s}-A)^{\vee}
containing the image of the map

(4.34)

\mu_{2}:H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{2}\to H^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A),

which is obtained by restricting the natural multiplication map

\mu_{K_{C}-2A,\;A+D_{s}}
of line bundles to the sub-vector space
H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{2}\subset H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A+D_{s})
, where
V_{2}
is a
2
-dimensional sub-vector space of
H^{0}(A+D_{s})
. We have the following natural multiplication/tensor maps

\displaystyle\mu:=\mu_{K_{C}-A,D_{s}}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes H^{0}(D_{s})\to H^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A),

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}:=\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A+D_{s}}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A+D_{s})\to H^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A)

\displaystyle\delta_{1}:=\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A}\otimes Id_{H^{0}(D_{s})}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(D_{s})\to H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes H^{0}(D_{s})

\displaystyle\delta_{2}:=Id_{H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)}\otimes\mu_{A,D_{s}}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(D_{s})\to H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A+D_{s})

Notice that
{\rm Im}(\mu\circ\delta_{1})={\rm Im}(\mu^{\prime}\circ\delta_{2})
and that
\delta_{2}
is surjective, from
h^{0}(D_{s})=1
and from the surjectivity of
\mu^{\prime}=\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A}
, as it follows from the base point free pencil trick (namely
{\rm Ker}(\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A})\simeq H^{0}(K_{C}-3A)
which is of dimension
g-3\nu+3
by Proposition
1.1
, whereas
\operatorname{dim}(H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A))=2(g-2\nu+2)
and
h^{0}(K_{C}-A)=h^{1}(A)=g+1-\nu
). Hence, any
v\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
of the form
\mathcal{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee}
, for
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,K_{C}-D_{s})
, contains
V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\subset H^{0}(A+D_{s})
in the cokernel of the map
\mu_{2}
as in (
4.34
); we want to show that
{\rm coker}(\mu_{2})=H^{0}(A)=V_{2}
.

Assume by contradiction that
\operatorname{dim}({\rm coker}(\mu_{2}))>2
; we can take any
H^{0}(A)\subset V_{3}\subseteq H^{0}(A+D_{s})
, where
\operatorname{dim}(V_{3})=3
. Since
h^{0}(A+D_{s})=\nu+s-g+1
, the family of sub-vector spaces
V_{3}
’s of
H^{0}(A+D_{s})
containing
H^{0}(A)
has dimension
\nu+s-g-1
. If we take
V_{3}
general among those containing
H^{0}(A)
, then the associated linear series
|V_{3}|
is base point free, because
|A|
is. Take
V_{2}^{\prime}\subset V_{3}
be a general hyperplane in
V_{3}
, which therefore also gives rise to a base-point-free pencil. Then we have the following maps, obtained by obvious restrictions
of a natural multiplication map,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{3}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{V_{3}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A),\\
\;\;\;\;\;\cup&&||\\
H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V^{\prime}_{2}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}-D_{s}-A)\\
\end{array}

Since
\operatorname{dim}(V_{3})=3
and
H^{0}(A)\subset V_{3}
, then
V_{3}=H^{0}(A+D^{\prime}_{s})
, for some effective divisor
D^{\prime}_{s}
s.t.
D_{s}-D^{\prime}_{s}\geq 0
and
\mbox{deg}(A+D^{\prime}_{s})\geq g+2
. Hence

\mu_{V_{2}^{\prime}}
is injective, therefore

\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V_{3}}))\geq\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}))\geq\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{2}))
. Since
\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}))=2(g-2\nu+2)=2g-4\nu+4
and
\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{2}))=2g-4\nu+4-\operatorname{dim}({\rm Ker}(\mu_{2}))=g-\nu+1
, the dimension of the family of hyperplanes of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,K_{C}-D_{s}-A)^{\vee}
containing the image of
\mu_{V_{3}}
is

h^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A)-\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V_{3}}))+\operatorname{dim}(\{V_{3}\})\leq h^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A)-(2g-4\nu+4)+(\nu+s-g-1).

This latter quantity
is less than
h^{0}(K_{C}+D_{s}-A)-(g-\nu+1)
if and only if
(g-3\nu+3)-(\nu+s-g-1)>0
which, certainly holds since
s\leq g-1
and
\nu<\frac{g+5}{4}
by assumptions. Since
{\rm corank}(\mu_{2})=2
, it follows that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
.

The fact that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
implies, from above, that the map
\mu_{\mathcal{F},A}
is surjective. Therefore

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F})\,h^{0}(A)-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A).

Now,
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
gives
h^{0}(\mathcal{F})=k_{3}=d-2g+5
whereas, since

h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=1
, Riemann-Roch gives
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A)=d+2\nu-2g+3
. Since
h^{0}(A)=2
, then from the previous formula we get

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=2(d-2g+5)-(d+2\nu-2g+3)=d-2g+7-2\nu
, as desired. Thus the proof of
(ii)
is complete.

(iii) and (iv) As above, we consider here vector bundles
\mathcal{F}
arising from extensions of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-A\to 0,

where
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
, where
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general with
3\nu-4\leq\mbox{deg}(N)=d-2g+2+\nu\leq g-2
, even if bundles
\mathcal{F}_{u}
are neither of
first type
nor of
second type
. This is done because the construction of the component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
, the estimate of its dimension, the proof that it is uniruled can be done similarly as in
(i)
and
(ii)
.

Indeed, we take
L:=K_{C}-A
and
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general.
We therefore get that
m:=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=h^{1}(N-L)=h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)=5g-5-d-2\nu
, since
\mbox{deg}(2K_{C}-N-A)=6g-6-d-2\nu>2g-2
, from the bounds on
\nu
. Using notation as in Theorem
3.1
, we see that
\ell:=h^{0}(L)=2g-2-\nu-g+3=g+1-\nu>3g-3-\nu-d=h^{1}(N):=r
if and only if
d\geq 2g-4
, which certainly holds in our bounds on
d
and
\nu
, and
m=5g-5-d-2\nu\geq\ell+1=g+2-\nu
if and only if
d\leq 4g-7-\nu
, which holds true. Computing
c(l,r,1)=l-r+1=(g-\nu+1)-(-d+3g-3-\nu)+1=d-2g+5
as in (
3.2
), from Theorem
3.1
, we get that

\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
is irreducible, of (expected) dimension

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{1})=m-c(\ell,r,1)=7g-10-2d-2\nu
.

Similarly as in the proof of Claim
4.24
, we see that for the line bundle
N
varying in a suitable dense subset
S\subseteq{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
, each

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
is of (constant) dimension
7g-11-2d-2\nu
; so we get an irreducible scheme, denoted by
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
, dominating
S
with fibers the
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
’s and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot})=g+(7g-11-2d-2\nu)=8g-11-2d-2\nu.

Notice that

(4.35)

\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot})-\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=8g-11-2d-2\nu-(10g-3d-18)=-2g+7+d-2\nu\geq 0,

where equality holds if and only if
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

To prove that the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
arising from a general pair
(N,[u])\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is stable we observe that since
d\leq 3g-4-\nu\leq 3g-6
,
setting
\delta:=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A)=2g-2-\nu
, we have that
2\delta-d=4g-4-2\nu-d\geq g+2-2\nu\geq 2
, so as in Proposition
1.16
, we take
\sigma:=1\;({\rm or}\;2)\equiv 2\delta-d{\mbox{ (mod 2) }}
; then
\sigma\leq 2\delta-d
because
2\delta-d\geq 2
and
\sigma\geq 4+d-2\delta=8+2\nu+d-4g
. Applying Proposition
1.16
to
X:=\varphi_{|K_{C}+L-N|}(C)
we have that, when
\sigma=1
i.e.
d
odd, one has

\operatorname{dim}\left(Sec_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d-1)}(X)\right)=(4g-4-2\nu-d-1)-1<\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=-2d+7g-2\nu-11

if and only if
d<3g-5
, which certainly holds in our bounds, when otherwise
\sigma=2
, i.e.
d
is even,

\operatorname{dim}\left(Sec_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d)}(X)\right)=(4g-4-2\nu-d)-1<\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=-2d+7g-2\nu-11

if and only if
d<3g-6
which is satisfied in our bounds on
d
. This shows that the general bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable.

From stability and from speciality
3
, the scheme
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
is endowed with a natural (rational) modular map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{d,\nu}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}&U^{s}_{C}(d)\\
(N,[u])&\longrightarrow&[\mathcal{F}_{u}]\end{array}

for which
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. Notice further that, for
N
general,
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is
unirational
: indeed, from the proof of
[
12
, Theorem 5.8]
, any such
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is dominated by an incidence variety
\mathcal{J}
which turns out to be a projective bundle over
\Sigma\simeq\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(K_{C}-N))
, whose fibers over
\Sigma
are projectve spaces of dimension
m-1-\ell>0
, where
m
and
\ell
as above. This implies that
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
is
uniruled
, being dominated by
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
which is uniruled over
S
.

We want to show that the map
\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational onto its image;
if so, we will get

(4.36)

\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu}))=\operatorname{dim}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot})=8g-11-2d-2\nu.

To prove that
\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational onto its image, we argue as in the proof of Claim
4.15
. Let
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
be general, so
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
for
(N,[u])\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}
general. Then

\pi^{-1}([\mathcal{F}_{u}])=\left\{(N^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}^{Tot}\,|\;\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{u}\right\}
. Assume by contradiction
(N^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])\in\pi^{-1}([\mathcal{F}_{u}])\setminus\{(N,[u])\}
; by determinantal reasons we must have
N^{\prime}=N
, i.e.
[u]\neq[u^{\prime}]
belong to the same
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N))
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable (so simple), this implies the existence of an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{F}_{u}
(which is not an endomorphism) giving rise to the following commutative diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccl}0\to&N&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}&\to&K_{C}-A&\to 0\\
&&&\downarrow^{\varphi}&&&\\
0\to&N&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{F}_{u}&\to&K_{C}-A&\to 0.\end{array}

The maps
\varphi\circ\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
determine, respectively, two non-zero global sections
s_{1}\neq s_{2}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})
. If these sections where linearly dependent, from
[
12
, Lemma 4.5]
we would get a contradiction of the fact that
[u]\neq[u^{\prime}]
by assumption. Thus the two sections must be linearly independent, in particular one must have
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})>1
. In Claim
4.25
below we are going to prove that, for
N
general and
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
general, this cannot occur deducing therefore that

\pi_{d,\nu}
is birational.

Claim 4.25
.

With above assumptions, let
N\in\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
be general and let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be such that it corresponds to a general element
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
. Let
F_{u}
denote the surface scroll associated to
\mathcal{F}_{u}
and let
\Gamma\subset F_{u}

be the section corresponding to the quotient line bundle
K_{C}-A
as in

(4.37)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-A\to 0.

Then the section
\Gamma\subset F_{u}
is linearly isolated (li) on the surface scroll
F_{u}
, i.e.
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})=1
.

Proof of Claim
4.25
.

The first assertion is equivalent to the second, by (
1.14
); therefore, we focus on proving the last assertion. Consider therefore the exact sequence obtained from (
4.37
) tensored by
A
, namely:

(4.38)

0\to N+A\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A\to K_{C}\to 0.

Notice that
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N+A)\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
, because they are both isomorphic to
H^{1}(N+A-K_{C})
. Recall that the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
arises by assumption from a general
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
. Consider

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}:=\left\{w\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N+A)\;|\;{\rm corank}(\partial_{w})\geq 1\right\}.

Applying Theorem
3.1
, straightforward computations show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is irreducible, of expected codimension

\widetilde{c(1)}:=h^{0}(K_{C})-h^{1}(N+A)+1
, as in (
3.2
), and also that for
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
general, one has
{\rm corank}(\partial_{w})=1
. Since one easily computes that

{\rm codim}_{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N+A)}(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1})=\widetilde{c(1)}>c(1)={\rm codim}_{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),

we therefore have
\operatorname{dim}(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1})<\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{1})
thus, for
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
general, the bundle

\mathcal{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A
corresponds to an element
v\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C},N+A)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
; in other words
\mathcal{F}_{v}
fits in (
4.38
) and it is such that the corresponding coboundary map
H^{0}(K_{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{v}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(N+A)
is surjective. This yields
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v})=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=h^{1}(K_{C})=1
so, by Serre duality,
1=h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes A^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})
, the last equality following from the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is of rank
2
with
\det(\mathcal{F}_{u})=K_{C}+N-A
.
∎

Consider the closure of
{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
in
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. From the above construction and the birationality of the map
\pi_{d,\nu}
, we have that
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is uniruled and of dimension
8g-11-2d-2\nu
. From (
4.35
), this dimension is certainly bigger than
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
for
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
, whereas it is equal to
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
if and only if
d=2g-7+2\nu
. Therefore, for
d\geq 2g-6+2\nu
any irreducible component
\mathcal{B}\subset B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
containing
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
has to be certainly
superabundant
.

To conclude that
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is actually a component, one needs to prove that
\operatorname{dim}(T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)))=8g-11-2d-2\nu,
where
T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d))
is the Zariski tangent space to
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
at the general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
. In particular, this will show that
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is a generically smooth component, which which will be also
regular
for
d=2g-7+2\nu
. From Remark
1.10
, such a Zariski tangent space is isomorphic to the cokernel of the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
thus

\operatorname{dim}(T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)))=\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}+\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))
. From (
4.35
), we are therefore left to proving

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu.

This is the point where the proofs of case (iii) and (iv) differ.

We first consider case (iii), proving the following result.

Claim 4.26
.

Let
g\geq 9
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+3}{4}
and
2g-7+3\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve. Then, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
general as above, one has
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
.

In particular,
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu
so
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(2-b)-mod}
which is generically smooth, uniruled and superabundant.

Proof of Claim
4.26
.

[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
general arises as
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
, with
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
general and
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general; with the use of (
4.38
), recall also that in Claim
4.25
we proved that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=1
. As in the proof of part (ii) above, we have the following long-exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u},\,A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(A)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)\to 0.

Since
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
,
h^{0}(A)=2
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=1
, we deduce that

h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})\geq 5
. Moreover,
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
corresponds to a hyperplane in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)^{\vee}\simeq H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)

containing the image of the map

(4.39)

\mu_{1}:H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes V_{1}\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A),

which is obtained by restricting the natural line-bundle multiplication map

\mu_{K_{C}-A,\;K_{C}-N}
to the sub-vector space
H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes V_{1}\subset H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
, where
V_{1}
is a
1
-dimensional sub-vector space of
H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
. Tensoring (
4.37
) by
A^{\vee}
we get

(4.40)

0\to N-A\to\mathcal{F}_{v}:=\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee}\to K_{C}-2A\to 0.

Since
h^{1}(K_{C}-2A)=h^{0}(2A)=3
, by Proposition
1.1
, and since

h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{v})\geq 5
from above, the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{v}
corresponds to an element

v\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}=\left\{w\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,N-A)\ |\ \operatorname{corank}(\partial_{w})\geq 2\right\}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,N-A).

From the description of such a degeneracy locus as in (
3.1
), it follows that
v
corresponds to a hyperplane in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,N-A)^{\vee}\simeq H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)
containing the image of the map

(4.41)

\mu_{2}:H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{2}\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A),

which is obtained by restricting the natural line bundle multiplication map
\mu_{K_{C}-2A,\;A+K_{C}-N}
to the sub-vector space
H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{2}\subset H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)
, where
V_{2}
is a
2
-dimensional sub-vector space of
H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)
.

Take
V_{2}:=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\subseteq H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)
. We have therefore the following multiplication/tensor maps

\displaystyle\mu_{1}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes V_{1}\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A),

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}:=\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A+K_{C}-N}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)

\displaystyle\delta_{1}:=\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A}\otimes Id_{V_{1}}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\to H^{0}(K_{C}-A)\otimes V_{1}

\displaystyle\delta_{2}:=Id_{H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)}\otimes{\mu_{A,K_{C}-N}}_{|_{H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}}}:

\displaystyle H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\to H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)

Note that
{\rm im}(\mu_{1}\circ\delta_{1})={\rm im}(\mu^{\prime}\circ\delta_{2})
, the latter being just a multiplication map. Moreover, notice that

\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A}
is surjective, as it follows from the base-point-free pencil trick (namely
{\rm Ker}(\mu_{K_{C}-2A,A})\simeq H^{0}(K_{C}-3A)
which is of dimension
g-3\nu+3
by Proposition
1.1
, whereas
\operatorname{dim}(H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes H^{0}(A))=2(g-2\nu+2)
and
h^{0}(K_{C}-A)=h^{1}(A)=g+1-\nu
) and so it obviously is also the map
\delta_{1}
. Hence any
v
as in (
4.40
) arising from
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
contains the vector space
V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}
in
{\rm coker}(\mu_{2})
.

We want to show that, under the assumptions, one has
{\rm coker}(\mu_{2})=V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\simeq H^{0}(A)
. Assume by contradiction that
\operatorname{dim}({\rm coker}(\mu_{2}))>2
; we can take any

3
-dimensional subspace
V_{3}
such that

V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\subset V_{3}\subseteq H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)

which is contained in
{\rm coker}(\mu_{2})
. Since
N
is general, so necessarily of degree
\mbox{deg}(N)\leq g-3
because
h^{1}(N)\geq 2
, we have
h^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)=\nu+(2g-2)-(d-2g+2+\nu)-g+1=3g-3-d
and
h^{0}(A)=2
, hence the family of
3
-dimensional subspaces
V_{3}
’s of
H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)
containing
V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}
has dimension
3g-5-d
. If we take
V_{3}
general among those containing
V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\simeq H^{0}(A)
, the associated linear series
|V_{3}|
is base-point-free because
|A|
is. Take
V_{2}^{\prime}\subset V_{3}
be a general hyperplane in
V_{3}
, which therefore also gives rise to a base-point-free pencil. We thus have the following maps, obtained by obvious restrictions of natural line bundle multiplication maps,

\begin{array}[]{ccc}H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V_{3}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{V_{3}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A),\\
\;\;\;\;\;\cup&&||\\
H^{0}(K_{C}-2A)\otimes V^{\prime}_{2}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A).\\
\end{array}

Since
\operatorname{dim}(V_{3})=3
and
V_{2}=H^{0}(A)\otimes V_{1}\subset V_{3}\subseteq H^{0}(A+K_{C}-N)
, we have that
V_{3}=H^{0}(A+D)
, for some effective divisor
D
s.t.
A+D\leq A+K_{C}-N
, i.e.
K_{C}-N-D
is effective, and
\mbox{deg}(A+D)\geq g+2
. Hence

\mu_{V_{2}^{\prime}}
is injective. Therefore

\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V_{3}}))\geq\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}))\geq\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{2}))
. Notice that
\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V^{\prime}_{2}}))=2(g-2\nu+2)=2g-4\nu+4
and
\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{2}))=2g-4\nu+4-\operatorname{dim}({\rm Ker}(\mu_{2}))=g-\nu+1
, hence the dimension of the family of hyperplanes of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-2A,K_{C}-D_{s}-A)^{\vee}=H^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)
containing the image of the map
\mu_{V_{3}}
is

h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)-\operatorname{dim}({\rm Im}(\mu_{V_{3}}))+\operatorname{dim}(\{V_{3}\})\leq h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)-(2g-4\nu+4)+(3g-5-d).

This latter quantity
is less than
h^{0}(2K_{C}-N-A)-(g-\nu+1)
if and only if
3g-5-d<g-3\nu+3
, namely
for
d>2g+3\nu-8
, as in our assumptions. This implies therefore
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
, since
{\rm corank}(\mu_{2})=2
, as desired.

To conclude the proof, notice that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
implies, from above, that the map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u},A}
is surjective hence
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\,h^{0}(A)-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)
. Now,
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
gives
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=k_{3}=d-2g+5
whereas, since
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=1
, Riemann-Roch gives
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=d+2\nu-2g+3
. From the fact that
h^{0}(A)=2
, we get

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=2(d-2g+5)-(d+2\nu-2g+3)=d-2g+7-2\nu
.
Since
H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\simeq{\rm ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u}})
we conclude that

[\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}]
general in
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is a
smooth point

of an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, which is therefore
generically smooth
. The
uniruledness
of such a component and the fact that it is
superabundant
are consequences of what we proved above, namely in (
4.35
) and in Claims
4.36
,
4.25
. Hence, the proof of Claim
4.26
is completed.
∎

The above arguments completes the proof of part (iii). Instead, to conclude the proof of part (iv), we are left to showing a similar result as in Claim
4.26
, but for
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
.

Claim 4.27
.

Let
g\geq 10
,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
and
2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Then, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
general as above, one has
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
.

In particular,
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2g+7-2\nu
so
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)}
which is generically smooth, uniruled and of dimension

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod})=8g-11-2d-2\nu\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}.

Moreover such a component is superabundant, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(2-b)-mod}
, when
d
is such that
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, whereas it is regular, i.e.
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3,(2-b)-mod}
, if
d=2g-7+2\nu
.

Proof of Claim
4.27
.

Recall that
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu})
general arises as
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
, with
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N)
general and
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general, so
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
, and that Claim
4.25
shows that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=1
. As in the proof of (ii), we have the following long-exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u},\,A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\otimes H^{0}(A)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)\to 0.

Since
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
,
h^{0}(A)=2
and
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=1
, we deduce that

h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})\geq 5
; as above we want to show that the equality holds.

If we set
\mathcal{E}:=\omega_{C}\otimes A\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee}
, by Serre duality, this reduces to showing that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=5=h^{0}(\mathcal{E})
. In the above notation, by Serre duality we have:

(4.42)

\displaystyle 1=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee}\otimes A^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))

\displaystyle 3=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{u}^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})

Furthermore, since
\mathcal{F}_{u}
fits in (
4.37
)
whereas
\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A
fits in (
4.38
), we deduce that
\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}
fits in

(4.43)

0\to A\to\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}\to K_{C}-N\to 0

whereas
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A)
fits in

(4.44)

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A)\to K_{C}-N-A\to 0

and
\mathcal{E}
fits in

(4.45)

0\to 2A\to\mathcal{E}\to K_{C}+A-N\to 0

Considering the natural multiplication map
H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{E})
and using the base-point-free pencil trick to get
{\rm ker}(\mu)\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))
, we see that
\mu
fits in

(4.46)

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))\to H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(\mathcal{E}),

from which we deduce that
{\rm rk}(\mu)=h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})h^{0}(A)-h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))=6-1=5
, as it follows from

h^{0}(A)=2
and from (
4.42
).

Thus, proving
h^{0}(\mathcal{E})=5=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})
reduces to showing the surjectivity of the multiplication map
\mu
as in (
4.46
). To do so, take (
4.43
), tensor it by
H^{0}(A)
and pass to cohomology to get

0\to H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(A)\to H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})\to H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C}-N);

considering the corresponding natural line bundle multiplication maps on the left, resp. on the right, side of the previous sequence together with their kernels, computed via the base-point-free pencil trick, we get the following commutative diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}&&0&&0&&0&&0&&\\[2.15277pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[2.15277pt]
0&\rightarrow&H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C})\simeq{\rm ker}(\mu_{A,A})&\rightarrow&H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))\simeq{\rm ker}(\mu)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{E},-2A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}-N-A)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{coker}({\mu_{\mathcal{E},-2A}})&\rightarrow&0\\[2.15277pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[2.15277pt]
0&\rightarrow&H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(A)&\rightarrow&H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C}-N)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{coker}({\mu_{\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}}})&\rightarrow&0\\[2.15277pt]
&&\;\;\;\;\;\;\downarrow^{\mu_{A,A}}&&\;\;\downarrow^{\mu}&&\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\downarrow^{\mu_{A,K_{C}-N}}&&\downarrow^{\mu_{\mathrm{coker}}}&&\\[2.15277pt]
0&\rightarrow&H^{0}(2A)&\rightarrow&H^{0}(\mathcal{E})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C}+A-N)&\rightarrow&\mathrm{coker}({\mu_{\mathcal{E}}})&\rightarrow&0\end{array}

where the upper-most (respectively, lower-most) horizontal sequence arises from
(
4.44
) (resp., (
4.45
)).

Since
h^{0}(2A)=3
, from Proposition
1.1
, whereas
h^{0}(A)=2
and
{\rm ker}(\mu_{A,A})\simeq\mathbb{C}
, we see that
\mu_{A,A}
is surjective. Furthermore, by (
4.42
), we have
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C})=h^{0}(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(-2A))=1
, so
h^{0}(K_{C}-N-A)=h^{0}(\mathrm{coker}(\mu_{\mathcal{E},-2A}))
. Since
{\rm rk}(\mu_{\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}})=2
, we have
\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{coker}(\mu_{\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}}))=2h^{0}(K_{C}-N)-2
. As for the map
\mu_{A,K_{C}-N}
, we see that
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-N-A)=4g-4-d-2\nu\geq 2g+4-5\nu\geq g-1
, from the assumptions
d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
and
\nu\leq\frac{g+5}{5}
. Since
N
is general, so it is
K_{C}-N-A
which is therefore non-special. This implies
h^{0}(K_{C}-N-A)=3g-3-d-2\nu
. Moreover, since
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-N)=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-N-A)+\nu
and
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-N+A)=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-N-A)+2\nu
, they are both non-special because they are general too. Therefore,

h^{0}(K_{C}-N)=3g-3-d-\nu
whereas
h^{0}(K_{C}-N+A)=3g-3-d
. From the third column of the previous diagram we see that

{\rm rk}(\mu_{A,K_{C}-N})=h^{0}(A)h^{0}(K_{C}-N)-h^{0}(K_{C}-N-A)=2(3g-3-d-\nu)-(3g-3-d-2\nu)=3g-3-d=h^{0}(K_{C}-N+A),

namely
\mu_{A,K_{C}-N}
is also surjective, which therefore implies that the map
\mu_{\mathrm{coker}}
is also surjective. Since
\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{coker}(\mu_{\mathcal{E},-2A}))=3g-3-d-2\nu
and

\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{coker}(\mu_{\mathcal{E}\otimes A^{\vee}}))=6g-8-2d-2\nu
, we get
\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{coker}(\mu_{\mathcal{E}}))=3g-5-d
, which gives
{\rm rk}(\mu_{\mathcal{E}})=2
. Therefore we get
h^{0}(\mathcal{E})=5=h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})
as desired.

To conclude the proof, notice that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=5
implies from above that the map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u},A}
is surjective hence
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})\,h^{0}(A)-h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)
. Since by Riemann-Roch
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
gives
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=k_{3}=d-2g+5
whereas
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=1
gives
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A)=d+2\nu-2g+3
, from the fact that
h^{0}(A)=2
, we get

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes A^{\vee})=2(d-2g+5)-(d+2\nu-2g+3)=d-2g+7-2\nu
.
Since
H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes A^{\vee})\simeq{\rm ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}_{u}})
we conclude that

[\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}]
general in
\overline{{\rm im}(\pi_{d,\nu})}
is a
smooth point

of an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
, which is therefore
generically smooth
. The
uniruledness
of such a component and the fact that it is
superabundant
, for
2g-6+2\nu\leq d\leq 2g-8+3\nu
, whereas it is
regular
, for
d=2g-7+2\nu
, are consequences of what we proved in (
4.35
) and in Claims
4.36
,
4.25
. Hence, the proof of Claim
4.27
is completed.
∎

The above arguments conclude the proof of (iv).
∎

\boxed{\mbox{Obstructions: no component from Case $(1-b)$}}
As for possible components filled-up by bundles of
first-type
arising from case
(1-b)
as in Lemma
4.3
, we already know from Propositions
4.9
-(i) and
4.13
-(1) that such bundles can never give rise to a component for, respectively,
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g}{3}
with
\frac{10}{3}g-5\leq d\leq 4g-5-2\nu
and
3\leq\nu\leq d\leq\frac{g}{2}
with
3g-6-\nu\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6
. For lower values of
d
, one has the following preliminary result.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
g\geq 7
and
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-4}{3}
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve. If
\mathcal{B}\subseteq B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
is an irreducible locus such that

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B})\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
and whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
is assumed to correspond to a rank-
2
, degree
d
stable bundle
\mathcal{F}
of speciality
i=h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=3
as in case
(1-b)
of Proposition
4.13
-
(1-b)
, then necessarily one has

(4.47)

\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu,

compatible with the given bounds on
\nu
.

Furthermore, for any
d
as in (
4.47
) and for any integer

\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g-2\nu-1
, there actually exist such irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}:=\mathcal{B}\subset B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}
presented as a general extension of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to K_{C}-(A+M)\to 0,

where
A
is the unique line bundle on
C
such that
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
,
M
is an effective divisor of movable points on
C
of degree
n:=\mbox{deg}(M)
, for some
\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g-2\nu-1
as above, and such that
A+M\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
is general in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
as in Lemma
4.2
, i.e.
|A+M|=g^{2}_{\nu+n}
is base-point-free and birationally very ample, and where furthermore
N
is general of its degree, s.t.
g+\nu-1\leq\mbox{deg}(N)\leq g+n-5
.

Proof.

Here we deal with case
(1-b)
as in Proposition
4.13
, where necessarily one has
2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu
. We are going to construct irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}\subset B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, whose general point arises as extensions

(u):\;\;0\to N\to\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-(A+M)\to 0,

where
\delta:=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-(A+M))
,
|A|=g^{1}_{\nu}
and

M=M_{2g-2-\delta-\nu}
is an effective divisor of movable points on
C
such that
|A+M|=g^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}
is base-point-free, with
A+M\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
general in such a component. We moreover show that, in order for such loci to be of dimension at least
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
, then (
4.47
) must be satisfied.

To ease notation, as in the statement, we will set
n=\mbox{deg}(M)=2g-2-\delta-\nu
. Notice that the assumption
n\geq\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}
is due to the fact that
\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
, from Lemma
4.3
-(1-b) and from the fact that
3\leq\nu<\frac{g}{2}
; recall indeed that the condition
\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
is necessary and sufficient for the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
to be not empty and of the expected dimension
3g-6-2\delta-\nu\geq 0
, by Lemma
4.2
. Moreover, since
h^{1}(K_{C}-A-M)=h^{0}(A+M)=3
, then
h^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)=\delta-g+4=g+2-\nu-n
and the latter is effective if and only if
n<g+2-\nu
. To sum up, we must have

\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n=\mbox{deg}(M)\leq g+1-\nu.

Similarly for
\delta
, using also (
4.3
), we get

g\leq\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
. In order to explicitly construct stable bundles, we consider the following:

Claim 4.29
.

In the above assumptions for
d
,
n
,
\nu
and
C
, let
\mathcal{F}_{u}
be a rank-
2
vector bundle arising as a general extension
u\in{\rm Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N)
, where
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)
is general. Then
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable and such that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=h^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M))=3
.

Proof of Claim
4.29
.

Set
L:=K_{C}-(A+M)
, so that
\mbox{deg}(L)=\delta
. Take
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-\delta}(C)
general; considering the condition
2\delta-d>0
, which is necessary for stability, one gets
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta<\delta=\mbox{deg}(L)
, i.e.
N\ncong L
, so from (
1.24
) one has

m:=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=2\delta-d+g-1
. By construction,
\ell:=h^{1}(L)=\delta-g+4=g+2-\nu-n
. Using Theorem
3.1
, one has

m\geq\ell+1
, indeed this reads
2\delta-d+g-1\geq g+3-\nu-n
which is equivalent to
2\delta-d\geq 4-\nu-n
, which certainly holds true from the bounds on
\nu
and
n
and from the fact that
2\delta-d
must be positive.

Let us consider now
r:=h^{1}(N)
, where
N
is general of its degree. If
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta<g-1
, then
h^{0}(N)=0
so, by Riemann-Roch,

r=h^{1}(N)=\delta-d+g-1=3g-3-\nu-n-d
which is positive for
d<\frac{5g-8-\nu}{2}
. Moreover, one certainly has

\ell=h^{0}(L)=\delta-g+4\geq r=\delta-d+g-1
, since
d\geq 2g-2
. In other words, if
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta<g-1
, then necessarily

2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu-n\leq\frac{5g-10-\nu}{2}
. If otherwise
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta\geq g-1
, by generality of
N
one has
r=h^{1}(N)=0
so

\ell\geq r
certainly holds in this case. Using the necessary condition on stability and the fact that
g\leq\delta\leq\frac{3g-6-\nu}{2}
, as it follows from (
4.3
) and Lemma
4.3
-(1-b), we have that
d\geq(g-1)+\delta
and also
d=\delta+(d-\delta)<2\delta
, i.e. in this case we have

2g-1\leq(g-1)+\delta\leq d\leq 2\delta-1\leq 3g-7-\nu
. In both cases, let

\mathcal{F}_{u}
be the bundle arising from a general
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N)
, i.e. it fits into the exact sequence

\begin{CD}&(u)&:0\to&N&\to&\ \ \mathcal{F}_{u}\ \ &\to\ &\ \ L:=K_{C}-A-M\ \ &\to 0\\
&\mbox{deg}&&d-2g+2+\nu+n&&d&&2g-2-\nu-n&\\
&h^{0}&&&&&&g-2\nu+2-b&\\
&h^{1}&&\operatorname{max}\{0,3g-3-\nu-n-d\}&&&&3&.\end{CD}

The previous computations and Theorem
3.1
show that the corresponding coboundary map
\partial_{u}
is surjective. Thus
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{u})=3
.

To prove stability of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
, for
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
, since
2\delta-d>0
, we have to consider two cases. If
2\delta-d\geq 2
, we can apply Proposition
1.16
, where
X=\varphi(C)
the image in
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))
via the morphism
\varphi
. From above one has
m=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=2\delta-d+g-1
so, for any integer
\sigma\leq g
, one has

\operatorname{dim}({\rm Sec}_{\frac{1}{2}(2\delta-d+\sigma-2)}(X)<m-1=\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)),

so
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable. If otherwise
2\delta-d=1
, then
d
is odd,
\delta=\mbox{deg}(L)=\frac{d+1}{2}
,
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta=\delta-1
and in such a case, one gets
m=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N))=g
. If we assume by contradiction that the general bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
is not stable, therefore there would exist a maximal sub-line bundle
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\rm max}\subset\mathcal{F}
such that
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})\geq\frac{d+1}{2}
, since
d
is odd. Because

\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})>\mbox{deg}(N)=d-\delta=\frac{d-1}{2}
, there is no line-bundle map from
\mathcal{M}
to
N
therefore, composing the inclusion
M\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
with the surjection
\mathcal{F}\to L\to 0
gives a line-bundle map
\mathcal{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\longrightarrow}}L
which must be an isomorphism, since
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{M})\geq\frac{d+1}{2}=\mbox{deg}(L)
. Thus, we would have that
L
is also a sub-line bundle of
\mathcal{F}
, i.e. the exact sequence (
1.22
) would split and
\mathcal{F}=L\oplus N
, so it would correspond to the zero-vector of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(L,N)
. This contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{F}
was chosen to be general in this
g
-dimensional vector space. Hence in any case, the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is also stable and the claim is proved.
∎

From the above arguments, for any integers
n
and
\nu
as above, one can consider a vector bundle

\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n}
on a suitable open, dense subset
S\subseteq\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)\times\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset\hbox{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)\times W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
, described by pairs

(N,A(M))
varying in
S
. Its rank is

m=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A-M,\,N))=2\delta-d+g-1=5g-5-d-2\nu-2n
as in (
1.24
). Taking the associated projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})\to S
(consisting of the family of projective spaces
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N)\right)
’s varying as the pair
(N,\,A+M)
varies in
S
) and since
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})=3g-6-2\delta-\nu=2n+\nu-g-2
by Lemma
4.2
,
one has

\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n}))=g+(5g-6-d-2\nu-2n)+(2n+\nu-g-2)=5g-8-d-\nu,

the latter being bigger than
2g-2
since, in any case, from above we must have
d\leq 3g-7-\nu
. From Claim
4.29
, for any integers
d
,
\nu
and
n
as above, one has therefore a natural (rational) modular map

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{d,\nu,n}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}

\displaystyle U_{C}(d)

\displaystyle(N,A+M,[u])\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{[}F_{u}],

which gives
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})\subseteq B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. If in particular
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})=3g-6-2\delta-\nu=2n+\nu-g-2=0
, then
\operatorname{dim}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})

has several components.

Claim 4.30
.

For any integers
d
,
\nu
and
n
and for
C
a general
\nu
-gonal curve as above, the map
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite onto its image. In particular,
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\delta,\nu})
is uniruled and
\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n}))=\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n}))=5g-8-d-\nu
.

Proof of Claim
4.30
.

The uniruledness of
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})
directly follows from the fact that it is dominated by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})
, which is ruled over
S
. Once we show that
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite, we will get also the statement about the dimension of
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})
.

To prove that
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite onto its image, let
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})
be general, so
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{u}
for
(N,A+M,u)\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})
general. Then

\pi^{-1}([\mathcal{F}_{u}])=\left\{(N^{\prime},A+M^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})\,|\;\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{u}\right\}
. Let
(N^{\prime},A+M^{\prime},[u^{\prime}])\in\pi^{-1}([\mathcal{F}_{u}])\setminus\{(N,A+M,[u])\}
.

If
A+M^{\prime}\simeq A+M
, namely
M
and
M^{\prime}
are linearly equivalent on
C
, then also
N^{\prime}=N
, since
K_{C}-A-M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}=\det(\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}})\simeq\det(\mathcal{F}_{u})=K_{C}-A-M+N
. Thus
[u]\neq[u^{\prime}]
belong to the same
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N))
; since
\mathcal{F}_{u}
is stable (so simple), this implies the existence of an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}{\to}\mathcal{F}_{u}
(which is not an endomorphism) giving rise to the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccl}0\to&N&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}&\to&K_{C}-(A+M)&\to 0\\
&&&\downarrow^{\varphi}&&&\\
0\to&N&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{F}_{u}&\to&K_{C}-(A+M)&\to 0.\end{array}

The maps
\varphi\circ\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
determine, respectively, two non-zero global sections
s_{1}\neq s_{2}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})
. These sections must be linearly dependent, otherwise one would have
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{u}\otimes N^{\vee})>1
which is a contradiction; indeed, from (
1.14
), this would imply that the section
\Gamma\subset F_{u}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
corresponding to the quotient
L:=K_{C}-(A+M)
of
\mathcal{F}_{u}
would be movable in a linear system but, from
d-\delta\geq\delta-g+1

as in assumptions, one gets

\mbox{deg}(L-N)=2\delta-d\leq g-1
thus the generality of
N
implies that also
L-N
is general of its degree, therefore
h^{0}(L-N)=0
and, by (
1.12
),
\Gamma
is therefore algebraically isolated (ai) (recall Definition
1.4
). On the other hand, if the two global sections
\varphi\circ\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
were linearly dependent,
[
12
, Lemma 4.5]
would give
[u]=[u^{\prime}]
, a contradiction.

If otherwise
A+M^{\prime}
is not isomorphic to
A+M
on
C
, then we set
L^{\prime}:=K_{C}-(A+M^{\prime})
, where
A+M^{\prime}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{2g-2-\delta}(C)
. Since
N^{\prime}+L^{\prime}=\det(\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}})\simeq\det(\mathcal{F}_{u})=L+N
, it follows that
N^{\prime}\simeq N+M^{\prime}-M
. One has therefore the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}&&&&0&&&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&\downarrow&&&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&N+M^{\prime}-M&&&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&\downarrow&&&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&N&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{u}\cong\mathcal{F}_{u^{\prime}}&\rightarrow&K_{C}-A-M&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&\downarrow&&&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&K_{C}-A-M^{\prime}&&&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&\downarrow&&&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&&&0&&&&\end{array}

Since
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-M-N)=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-2M^{\prime}+M-N)
, the generality of
N
implies that they are both general so, as above,
{\rm Div}^{1,\delta}_{F_{u}}
consists of finitely many reduced points.

Previous arguments show that, in any case, the map
\pi_{d,\nu,n}
is generically finite onto its image, and the Claim is proved.
∎

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
denote the closure of
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})
in
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
is
uniruled
, being finitely dominated by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{d,\nu,n})
, which is ruled over
S
, and such that
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})=5g-8-d-\nu
, as
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{d,\nu,n})
is dense in it. Notice first that, for any
\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g+1-\nu
as above, the dimension of the locus
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
does not depend on
n=\mbox{deg}(M)
; furthermore notice that

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})=5g-8-d-\nu\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d\;\;\Leftrightarrow\;\;d\geq\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2},

proving the lower-bound on
d
as in (
4.47
) (recall that the upper-bound is given by what proved for case
(1-b)
in Proposition
4.9
and
4.13
). From above, one has therefore the following situation:

(i)

when
N
is general with
\mbox{deg}(N)<g-1
, from the consequent bounds on
d
imposed by this choice, condition
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})\geq\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
gives
d\leq 3g-4-\nu-n\leq\frac{5g-10-\nu}{2}<\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}
, which is ruled-out from the chosen range for
d
. In other words, the case
\mbox{deg}(N)<g-1
can never give rise to a locus
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
of dimension at least
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
;

(ii)

when otherwise
\mbox{deg}(N)\geq g-1
, which is general of its degree, we then have
\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu
as in (
4.47
) and, for any choice of admissible
n
, the constructed loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
have dimension at least
\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}=10g-18-3d
, where the equality holds iff
d=\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}
.

The above arguments conclude the proof of Lemma
4.28
.
∎

Once the irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
have been constructed, we need to control the Zariski tangent space of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
at a general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
. This is done in the following:

Claim 4.31
.

For
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
general as above, the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is such that
\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=d-2\nu-2g+7
. In particular, we have

\operatorname{dim}(T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)))=\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}+\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=8g-2d-2\nu-11>\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})=5g-8-d-\nu.

Proof of Claim
4.31
.

Recall that
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
general arises as an extension of the line bundles
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+2\nu}(C)
general and
L=K_{C}-(A+M)
. In particular, since
h^{1}(N)=0
, then

H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\simeq H^{0}(N)\oplus H^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)\;{\rm and}\;H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\simeq H^{0}(A+M).

Thus the Petri map
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
reads also as

\begin{CD}\left[H^{0}(N)\oplus H^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)\right]&\;\otimes\;&\;H^{0}(A+M)&\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\;\;&H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}).\\
\end{CD}

By Claim
4.29
, we have
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})=h^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M))=h^{0}(A+M)=3
. Consider the following diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}&&0&&0&&0&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&N+A+M-K_{C}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}\otimes(A+M-K_{C})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{C}&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&N\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{(}K_{C}-A-M)\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{C}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}\otimes N^{\vee}&\rightarrow&(K_{C}-A-M)\otimes N^{\vee}&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&0&&0&&0&&\end{array}

which arises from the exact sequence defining
\mathcal{F}
and from its dual sequence. If we tensor the column in the middle by
\omega_{C}
, we get
H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes(A+M))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
. From the isomorphism
H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\simeq H^{0}(A+M)
, the Petri map of
\mathcal{F}
simply reads

\mu_{\mathcal{F}}:H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(A+M)\to H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
. By the previous injection

H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes(A+M))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
we deduce that the kernel of
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}
is the same as the kernel of the natural multiplication map
\mu^{\prime}:=\mu_{\mathcal{F},A+M}:H^{0}(\mathcal{F})\otimes H^{0}(A+M)\to H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes(A+M))
.

Since
N
is general with
h^{1}(N)=0
, so it is
N+A+M
and thus
h^{1}(N+A+M)=0
. In particular, from the above diagram one has

H^{0}(N+A+M)\oplus H^{0}(\omega_{C})\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes(A+M))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee}),

where the isomorphism follows from the first row whereas the inclusion comes from the middle column of the previous diagram.

Considering the natural multiplication map

\mu_{N,A+M}:H^{0}(N)\otimes H^{0}(A+M)\to H^{0}(N+A+M)
and the Petri map of
A+M
, namely
\mu_{A+M}:H^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)\otimes H^{0}(A+M)\to H^{0}(K_{C})
, from above we have that
\mu_{\mathcal{F}}=\mu_{N,A+M}\oplus\mu_{A+M}
and we can consider the following exact diagram:

{\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{cclcccl}&0&&0&&0&\\[3.44444pt]
&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[3.44444pt]
0\to&\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{N,A+M})&\to&H^{0}(N)\otimes H^{0}(A+M)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{N,A+M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(N+A+M)&\\[3.44444pt]
&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[3.44444pt]
0\to&\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}})&\to&[H^{0}(N)\oplus H^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)]\otimes H^{0}(A+M)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\mathcal{F}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(\mathcal{F}\otimes(A+M))&\subset H^{0}(\omega_{C}\otimes\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\\[3.44444pt]
&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[3.44444pt]
0\to&\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{A+M})&\to&H^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)\otimes H^{0}(A+M)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{A+M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&H^{0}(K_{C})&\\[3.44444pt]
&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[3.44444pt]
&0&&0&&0&\end{array}}

To compute the kernel of the multiplication map
\mu_{N,A+M}
,
recall that
A+M
is globally generated (cf. Lemma
4.2
-
(b)
), therefore we have the exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{K}}\to H^{0}(A+M)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}\to A+M\to 0,

where
\mathcal{K}
denotes the
rank-
2
kernel vector bundle of the previous surjection. Tensoring by
\mathcal{F}
, one can complete to the following commutative diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccccccccccc}&&0&&0&&0&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&(-A)\otimes N&\rightarrow&H^{0}(A)\otimes N&\rightarrow&A\otimes N&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&\mathcal{K}\otimes N&\rightarrow&H^{0}(A+M)\otimes N&\rightarrow&N\otimes(A+M)&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[4.30554pt]
0&\rightarrow&N-M&\rightarrow&N&\rightarrow&N\otimes\mathcal{O}_{M}&\rightarrow&0\\[4.30554pt]
&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\downarrow&&\\[4.30554pt]
&&0&&0&&0&&.\end{array}

By the bounds
\mbox{deg}(N)\geq g-1+\nu
and by generality of
N
, we have
h^{1}(N-A)=0
. Since
h^{0}(N-M)=0
, we get

\operatorname{dim}\left(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{N,A+M})\right)=h^{0}(N-A)=(d-2g+2+\nu+n-g+1)-\nu=d-3g+3+n.

On the other hand the Petri map
\mu_{A+M}
has a corank
2n+\nu-g-2
, since
\operatorname{dim}(\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}})=3g-6-2\delta-\nu=2n+\nu-g-2
, thus

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{A+M}))=3\,h^{0}(K_{C}-A-M)-g+(2n+\nu-g-2)=3(g+2-\nu-n)-g+(2n+\nu-g-2)=g+4-n-2\nu.

Hence, from above, we get

\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{\mathcal{F}}))=(d-3g+3+n)+(g+4-n-2\nu)=d-2g-2\nu+7
, as desired.
∎

We want to show that the irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
constructed above,
which actually exist under conditions as in Lemma
4.28
, namely

3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-4}{3},\;\;\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g-2\nu-1,

are strictly contained (according to the chosen
d
) as proper closed subschemes in the irreducible components of the form
\mathcal{B}_{\rm sup,3,(2-b)-mod}
as in
Proposition
4.22
-
(iii)
and
(iv)
, which instead have been constructed therein for any

3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g+3}{3}\;\;{\rm and}\;\;2g-7+2\nu\leq d\leq 3g-4-\nu.

Notice that when we take
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-4}{3}
, as requested for the existence of the irreducible loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}

in Lemma
4.28
, one clearly has
3g-7-\nu<3g-4-\nu
and
2g-7+2\nu<\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}
;
therefore, for any irreducible locus

\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
there certainly exists an irreducible component of type
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
as in Proposition

4.22
-
(iii)
and
(iv)
. We moreover recall that

\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod})=8g-11-2\nu-2d>5g-8-d-\nu=\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n})

and that for

[\mathcal{F}_{w}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
general we have also

\operatorname{dim}(T_{[\mathcal{F}]}(B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)))=8g-2d-2\nu-11=\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}),

as proved in Claim
4.31
. Recall further that a general point

[\mathcal{F}_{u}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
as in Proposition
4.22
-
(iii)
and
(iv)
arises from an exact sequence of the form

(4.48)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}_{u}\to K_{C}-A\to 0,

where
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
general,
h^{1}(K_{C}-A)=h^{0}(A)=2
and
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,\,N)
general, namely the associated coboundary map
\partial_{u}
is s.t.
{\rm corank}(\partial_{u})=1
, whereas
[\mathcal{F}_{w}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
general as in Lemma
4.28
arises from a general extension

(4.49)

0\to N^{\prime}\to\mathcal{F}_{w}\to K_{C}-(A+M)\to 0,

where
N^{\prime}\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)
general,
M
is an effective divisor of movable points of degree
\mbox{deg}(M)=n
on
C
s.t.
A+M\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
is general in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
as in Lemma
4.2
, i.e.

|A+M|=g^{2}_{\nu+n}
is base-point-free and birationally very ample, so
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{w})=h^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M))=h^{0}(A+M)=3
and the corresponding coboundary map
\partial_{w}
is surjective.

Proposition 4.32
.

Let
3\leq\nu\leq\frac{g-4}{3}
,
\frac{5g-10+\nu}{2}\leq d\leq 3g-7-\nu
and
\frac{g+2-\nu}{2}\leq n\leq g-2\nu-1
be integers and let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g
. Then any irreducible locus
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
as in Lemma
4.28
is strictly contained in a component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
as in Proposition
4.22
, in such a way that
[\mathcal{F}_{w}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
general is a smooth point of the corresponding component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
containing it.

In particular, the loci
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
arising from case
(1-b)
as in Lemma
4.3
can never fill-up an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
.

Proof.

Take
[\mathcal{F}_{w}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
a general point, for some
d
and
n
as in the statement; thus as already observed
\mathcal{F}_{w}
fits in (
4.49
), where
N^{\prime}\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)
general,
M
is an effective divisor of movable points of degree
\mbox{deg}(M)=n
on
C
s.t.
A+M\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subset W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
is general in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
as in Lemma
4.2
and
w\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),N^{\prime})
is general, in particular the coboundary map
\partial_{w}
is surjective.

In terms of the associated surface scroll
F_{w}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{w})
, we observe that
F_{w}
contains a special section
\Gamma\subset F_{w}
, of speciality
i(\Gamma)=3
and degree
\mbox{deg}(\Gamma)=2g-2-\nu-n
, corresponding to the line bundle quotient
K_{C}-(A+M)
, which is algebraically isolated (ai) in
F_{w}
: indeed, from (
1.12
),
{\mathcal{N}}_{\Gamma/F_{w}}\simeq K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime}
which is general, by generality of
N^{\prime}
, of degree
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime})=4g-4-2n-2\nu-d
which, from the bounds on
d
and
n
, is such that
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime})\leq\frac{g-2-3\nu}{2}
so it is not effective by generality, i.e.
h^{0}({\mathcal{N}}_{\Gamma/F_{w}})=0
.

Composing the line-bundle injection
N^{\prime}(-M)\hookrightarrow N^{\prime}
with the exact sequence (
4.49
) shows that
N^{\prime}(-M)
is also a sub-line bundle of
\mathcal{F}_{w}
. This gives the exact sequence

(4.50)

0\to N^{\prime}(-M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\sigma}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}_{w}\to\mathcal{Q}:=\frac{\mathcal{F}_{w}}{N^{\prime}(-M)}\to 0,

where
\mathcal{Q}
is the quotient sheaf of
\tilde{\sigma}
. Notice that the injective line-bundle map
\tilde{\sigma}
gives rise to a global section
\sigma
of the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}
as in the following exact sequence:

(4.51)

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)\to\mathcal{Q}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)\to 0.

If we tensor (
4.49
) by
(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)
we get

0\to M\to\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)\to K_{C}-A-N^{\prime}\to 0;

notice that
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-N^{\prime})=4g-4-d-2\nu-n
which, from the bounds on
d
and
\nu
, is such that
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-N^{\prime})\leq g+1-2\nu\leq g-5
so, as above, it is not effective
by the generality of
N^{\prime}
. This implies that

(4.52)

h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M))=h^{0}(M).

Claim 4.33
.

For
A+M\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{\nu+n}(C)
general in the component
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
as in Lemma
4.2
-
(b)
, one has
h^{0}(M)=1
.

Proof of Claim
4.33
.

Since
M
is effective, then certainly one has
h^{0}(M)\geq 1
. Moreover
h^{0}(M)\leq 2
, because
h^{0}(A+M)=3
and

|A+M|
is base-point-free. We want to show that
h^{0}(M)=2
cannot occur.

If by contradiction we had
h^{0}(M)=2
, considering the natural multiplication map

\mu_{A,M}:H^{0}(A)\otimes H^{0}(M)\to H^{0}(A+M)

and since
|A|
is base-point-free, by the base-point-free pencil trick we would get
{\rm ker}(\mu_{A,M})=h^{0}(M-A)\geq 1
because
h^{0}(A+M)=3
. Thus there would exist an effective divisor
D_{n-\nu}
of degree
\mbox{deg}(D_{n-\nu})=n-\nu
such that
M\sim A+D_{n-\nu}
so that

A+M\sim 2A+D_{n-\nu}
, contradicting that
A+M
is general in
\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,1}}}
.
∎

Thus, from (
4.52
) and Claim
4.33
, we get
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M))=1
. Similarly, tensoring (
4.49
) by
(N^{\prime})^{\vee}
we get

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\to K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime}\to 0.

Since
K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime}
is general, by generality of
N^{\prime}
, and since
\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime})=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-N^{\prime})-n<\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A-N^{\prime})
, reasoning as above we have

h^{0}(K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime})=0
, so
h^{0}(\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee})=h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{C})=1
.

In other words, setting
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)
, from (
4.52
) and from above we have that

h^{0}(\mathcal{E})=h^{0}(\mathcal{E}(-M))=1.

Therefore, the unique global section
\sigma
of
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)
as in (
4.51
) coincides with the unique global section of
\mathcal{E}(-M)=\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}
which, from (
4.49
), does not vanish at any point of
C
as the cokernel of the injection
\mathcal{O}_{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}(-M)=\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}
is locally free, being isomorphic to
K_{C}-A-M-N^{\prime}
. In other words, the global section
\sigma
as in (
4.51
) vanishes along
M
and we have

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}_{w}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)\to(K_{C}-A-N^{\prime})\oplus\mathcal{O}_{M}\to 0.

Comparing with (
4.51
), we have therefore
\mathcal{Q}\simeq(K_{C}-A-M)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{M}
and, replacing it into (
4.50
), we get that
\mathcal{F}_{w}
fits also in

(4.53)

0\to N^{\prime}(-M)\to\mathcal{F}_{w}\to(K_{C}-(A+M))\oplus\mathcal{O}_{M}\to 0,

i.e. the surface scroll
F_{w}
contains the reducible unisecant
\widetilde{\Gamma}:=\Gamma+f_{M}
as in (
1.10
) of degree
\mbox{deg}(\widetilde{\Gamma})=2g-2-\nu
and of speciality
i(\widetilde{\Gamma})=i(\Gamma)=3
as in (
1.15
), being
\Gamma
the section corresponding to the quotient line bundle
K_{C}-(A+M)
.

From (
4.53
), we see that
\mathcal{F}_{w}
arises therefore also as an element
w
of the extension space

(4.54)

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}((K_{C}-(A+M))\oplus\mathcal{O}_{M},\;N^{\prime}(-M))\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{M},\;N^{\prime}(-M))\oplus\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),\;N^{\prime}(-M)).

We have
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-(A+M),\;N^{\prime}(-M))\simeq H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})
whereas, from
[
20
, III. Proposition 6.7, p. 235]
and from the fact that

\omega_{C}
and
N^{\prime}(-M)
are locally free, we have that

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{M},\;N^{\prime}(-M))\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{M}\otimes\omega_{C},\;N^{\prime}(-M)\otimes\omega_{C})\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{M}\otimes\omega_{C}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M),\;\omega_{C});

the latter is such that

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{M}\otimes\omega_{C}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M),\;\omega_{C})\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M}\otimes\omega_{C}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M))^{\vee}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M})^{\vee}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M}),

where the first isomorphism follows from
[
20
, III. Theorem 7.6, p. 243]
, the second and the third isomorphism follow from the fact that
\omega_{C}\otimes(N^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{C}(M)
is locally free whereas
\mathcal{O}_{M}
is a sky-scraper sheaf. Thus, replacing in (
4.54
), we get that

(4.55)

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}((K_{C}-(A+M))\oplus\mathcal{O}_{M},\;N^{\prime}(-M))\simeq H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})\oplus H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M});

this means we have the following exact sequence

(4.56)

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M})\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1}((K_{C}-(A+M))\oplus\mathcal{O}_{M},\;N^{\prime}(-M))\to H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})\to 0.

Similarly, since
N\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu}(C)
and
N^{\prime}\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+2+\nu+n}(C)
are both general, we may consider
N=N^{\prime}-M
, therefore for
[\mathcal{F}_{u}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,\,(2-b)-mod}
general, we have

u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,\,N^{\prime}-M)\simeq H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}-M).

If we consider the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}(-M)\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\to\mathcal{O}_{M}\to 0
and tensor it by

N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}
, we get

0\to N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}-M\to N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}\to\mathcal{O}_{M}\otimes(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{M}\to 0.

Notice that, from the bounds on
d
and
n
,
\mbox{deg}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})=(d-2g+2+n+\nu)+\nu-(2g-2)=d-4g+4+n+2\nu\leq(3g-7-\nu)-4g+4+(g-2\nu-1)+2\nu=-4-\nu<0
, therefore
h^{0}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})=0
and the previous exact sequence gives in cohomology

(4.57)

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M})\to H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}-M)\to H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})\to 0.

Comparing (
4.56
) and (
4.57
) we deduce the following geometric interpretation via
elementary transformations
of rank-
2
vector bundles, which will complete the proof: notice first that
H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N^{\prime})
and
that any
z\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N^{\prime})
gives rise to a rank-
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{z}
of degree
\mbox{deg}(\mathcal{F}_{z})=\mbox{deg}(N^{\prime})+\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A)=d+n
(moreover, for
z\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N^{\prime})
general, one has also
h^{1}(\mathcal{F}_{z})=h^{1}(K_{C}-A)=2
). Let
F_{z}:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{z})
be the surface scroll corresponding to any such a bundle; hence
F_{z}
contains a special section
\Gamma^{\prime}
, of speciality
2
and of degree
\mbox{deg}(\Gamma^{\prime})=\mbox{deg}(K_{C}-A)=2g-2-\nu
corresponding to the quotient line bundle
K_{C}-A
of
\mathcal{F}_{z}
. Moreover, since from above
h^{0}(K_{C}-N^{\prime}-A)=0
, we deduce that the section
\Gamma^{\prime}
is algebraically isolated (ai) (so fixed) on the surface scroll
F_{z}
.

Looking at (
4.57
), we see that any
u\in H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}-M)\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,\>N^{\prime}-M)
gives rise to a surface scroll
F_{u}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}_{u})
obtained
by an elementary transformation of a surface scroll
F_{z}
as above, for
z\in H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C})\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,\,N^{\prime})
where such an elementary
transformation is performed by considering
n
points
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}\in F_{z}
, which sit on the (distinct) fibers
f_{M}
of
F_{z}
over some
M\in C^{(n)}

and which do not belong to the special section
\Gamma^{\prime}
and such an elementary transformation is obtained by first blowing-up
F_{z}
at each point of intersection between the fiber
f_{q_{i}}
and the
special section
\Gamma^{\prime}
and then by contracting the proper transform of the fiber
f_{q_{i}}
, for any
1\leq i\leq n
. This elementary transformation gives rise to a surface scroll
F_{u}
arising from
u\in H^{1}(N^{\prime}+A-K_{C}-M)\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N^{\prime}-M)
and, from the fact that
H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{M})
is the kernel of the surjection (
4.57
), it follows that the same surface scroll
F_{z}
gives rise, by moving points
M
on
C
and by performing the elementary transformation as above, to positive dimensional families of surface scrolls
F_{u}
.
Since for both
u
and
z
general the speciality of the corresponding bundles is
2
, by speciality reasons, it is clear that the same arguments apply for
z\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}_{1}}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,\,N^{\prime})
and
u\in\mathcal{W}_{1}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(K_{C}-A,N^{\prime}-M)
general, which both give rise instead to bundles of speciality
3
.

By moving along the fibers
f_{M}
of
F_{z}
the points
q_{1},\ldots,q_{n}
of the elementary transformations described above and letting them specialize to the points of intersection between the fibers
f_{M}
with the fixed section
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
F_{z}
, performing the elementary transformation centered along the specialized points gives rise to a surface scroll
F_{w}
arising from a bundle
\mathcal{F}_{w}
fitting in (
4.49
), whose presentation is also equivalent to that in (
4.53
). This implies that
F_{u}

degenerates to
F_{w}
so also
\mathcal{F}_{u}
degenerates to
\mathcal{F}_{w}
, which shows that
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}\subset\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
, according to the chosen degree
d
.

Finally, the fact that
[\mathcal{F}_{w}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,n}
general is a smooth point for
\mathcal{B}_{\rm 3,(2-b)-mod}
follows from the computations in Claim
4.31
and the proof is complete.
∎

\boxed{2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6:\mbox{Construction of regular components via bundles of {second type}}}
In the steps previously described, a part from
d=2g-6+2\nu
, as in Proposition
4.14
-
(iii)
, and
d=2g-7+2\nu
, as in Proposition
4.22
-
(iv)
, we have always constructed
superabundant
components of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. In this section, our aim will be therefore to construct
regular
components of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. For this reason, we must limit here to

2g-2\leq d\leq\frac{10}{3}g-6,

because for
d\geq\frac{10}{3}g-5
no component on
expected dimension

\rho_{d}^{k_{3}}
(in particular,
regular
) can actually exist (recall Remark
4.7
).

Since from Propositions
4.8
and
4.32
we know that both cases
(1-b)
and
(1-c)
as in Lemma
4.3
can never give rise to an irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
at all, whereas in Proposition
4.14
the case of bundles on type
(1-a)
has been fully investigated in any possible degree
d
(recall that the only case of a
regular
component whose general point arises as a bundle of type
(1-a)
is for
d=2g-6+2\nu
),
we will concentrate on bundles of
second type
, in the sense of Definition
1.15
,
or on some of their natural modifications, whether such variations make our construction drastically simplified, as it already happened in the proof of Proposition
4.22
-
(ii)
,
(iii)
and
(iv)
. If
\mathcal{B}
is any possible irreducible component of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
whose general point
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}
corresponds to a bundle of
second type
, i.e. fitting in an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-D)\to 0\;,

for some divisor
D\geq 0
for which
\omega_{C}(-D)
effective with
h^{1}(\omega_{C}(-D))=1
, from the fact that
h^{1}(\mathcal{F})
must be
3
it is clear that
N
has to be special, of speciality at least
2
. Below we will take care of the most natural case, namely
D=0
, i.e.
\mathcal{F}
arising from an exact sequence of the form

(4.58)

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0.

Accordingly, we will investigate on families of stable, rank-
2
and degree
d
vector bundles fitting in (
4.58
) and which fill-up a
regular
irreducible component of
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
. Precisely, in what follows we will prove the existence of a
regular
component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
of
B^{k_{3}}_{d}\cap U^{s}_{C}(d)
, such that
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
fits in (
4.58
) where

•

for
2g-2\leqslant d\leqslant 3g-7
,
N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+4}(C)
is general,

•

for
3g-6\leqslant d\leqslant\frac{10g}{3}-6
,
N
is s.t.
\omega_{C}\otimes N^{\vee}=g^{2}_{t}\in\overline{W^{\vec{w}_{2,0}}}\subseteq W^{2}_{t}(C)
is general as in Lemma
4.2
.

\boxed{2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-7:\;\mbox{existence of a regular component}}
We first focus on
2g-2\leq d\leq 3g-7
. As it will be clear from the proof of Proposition
4.34
below, to verify the stability of constructed bundles
\mathcal{F}
as in (
4.58
), we will further split our analysis into two cases, namely

2g-2\leq d\leq\lfloor\frac{5g}{2}\rfloor-6\;\;\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\;\lfloor\frac{5g}{2}\rfloor-5\leq d\leq 3g-7.

The main difference will be that, when
\lfloor\frac{5g}{2}\rfloor-5\leq d\leq 3g-7
, the stability of a general
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
will be a consequence of the stability of a specific vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{w}
as in the proof of Claim
4.38
, i.e. such that
[\mathcal{F}_{w}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
is a
special
point of such a component as in (Case 2) of the proof of Claim
4.37
, and the fact that stability is an open condition in irreducible families.

Proposition 4.34
.

Let
C
be a general
\nu
-gonal curve of genus
g\geqslant 7
, with
3\leqslant\nu<\frac{g}{2}
.

For any integer
2g-2\leqslant d\leqslant 3g-7
, one has that
B_{d}^{k_{3}}\cap U_{C}^{s}(d)
contains a regular component
\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
which is uniruled and, for
[\mathcal{F}]\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm reg,3}
general, the corresponding bundle
\mathcal{F}
fits into an exact sequence of the form

0\to N\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}\to 0,

where

\bullet

N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+2}(C)
is general (special, non-effective),

\bullet

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{v}
with
v
general in an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
of
\mathcal{W}_{2}\subset\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)
as in (
3.1
), such that
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
has the expected codimension
c(2):=2d-10-4g
, as in (
3.2
), and
v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is s.t.
{\rm corank}(\partial_{v})=2
;

Furthermore
\mathcal{F}
admits as quotient line bundles also
\omega_{C}(-p)
, for
p\in C
general, so it also fits in the exact sequence
0\to N(p)\to\mathcal{F}\to\omega_{C}(-p)\to 0
, where
p\in C
and
N(p)\in{\rm Pic}^{d-2g+3}(C)
are general.

If
\Gamma
, resp.
\Gamma_{p}
, denotes a section in the surface scroll
F:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
which corresponds to the quotient line bundle
\omega_{C}
, resp.
\omega_{C}(-p)
with
p\in C
general, then:

(i)

\Gamma
is not linearly isolated (not li) on
F
, as it moves in a
2
-dimensional linear system, and
\omega_{C}
is not of minimal degree among possible special, effective quotients of
\mathcal{F}
. In particular
\mathcal{F}
is neither rigidly presented (not rp) nor rigidly specially presented (not rsp) via the canonical quotient
\omega_{C}
;

(ii)

\Gamma_{p}
is neither algebraically isolated (not ai) nor algebraically specially isolated (not asi) on
F
, i.e.

\mathcal{F}
is neither rigidly presented (not rp) nor rigidly specially presented (not rsp) via
\omega_{C}(-p)
. Moreover, the line bundle
\omega_{C}(-p)
is not of minimal degree among possible special quotients of
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Let us consider, in notation (
1.22
),
L:=\omega_{C}
and
N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+2}(C)
general which, from the bounds on
d
, is non-effective and of speciality
r:=h^{1}(N)=3g-3-d\geqslant 2
, whereas
\ell:=h^{0}(L)=g
and
m:=\operatorname{dim}(\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N))=5g-5-d
. From the given bounds on
d
, one easily sees that
\ell\geq r
and
m\geq\ell+1
so, from Theorem
3.1
, for a general
u\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)
, the corresponding coboundary map
\partial_{u}
is surjective and the degeneracy locus
\mathcal{W}_{1}\subsetneq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)
is irreducible, of the expected codimension
c(1)=(\ell-r+1)
, as in (
3.2
), moreover its general point
z\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
corresponds to a bundle
\mathcal{F}_{z}
for which
the associated coboundary map
\partial_{z}
is such that
{\rm corank}(\partial_{z})=1
.

This in particular implies that
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{W}_{2}\subsetneq\mathcal{W}_{1}
, where the strict inclusion follows from the very definition of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
for which for any
v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}
one has
{\rm corank}(\partial_{v})\geq 2
(whereas, from above,
z\in\mathcal{W}_{1}
general has instead the corresponding coboundary map
\partial_{z}
of corank
1
) and the non-emptyness of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
follows from the fact that the trivial bundle
\mathcal{F}_{0}=N\oplus\omega_{C}
, corresponding to the zero-vector of the extension space, certainly is an element of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, because its associated coboundary map
\partial_{0}
is the zero map and
r=h^{1}(N)=3g-3-d\geq 4
.

From (
3.2
), the
expected codimension
of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is
c(2)=2(\ell-r+2)=2d+10-4g\geq 6
, as
d\geq 2g-2
. We want to prove that there exists at least an irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{2}
which is of the
expected dimension
, namely
m-c(2)=5g-5-d-(2d+10-4g)=9g-15-3d
, whose general point
v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is such that
{\rm corank}(\partial_{v})=2
. To do this, we first need the following preliminary result:

Claim 4.35
.

With assumptions as above, let
N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+2}(C)
be general and let
\mathbb{G}:=\mathbb{G}(2,H^{0}(K_{C}-N))
be the Grassmannian parametrizing
2
-dimensional sub-vector spaces in
H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
, where the latter is of dimension
r=3g-3-d>2
. Let
\mu:=\mu_{K_{C}-N,\;K_{C}}:H^{0}(K_{C}-N)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C})\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N)
be the natural multiplication map and, for any
[V_{2}]\in\mathbb{G}
, let
\mu_{V_{2}}
be the restriction of the map
\mu
to the sub-vector space
V_{2}\otimes H^{0}(K_{C})\subset H^{0}(K_{C}-N)\otimes H^{0}(K_{C})
, namely
\mu_{V_{2}}:V_{2}\otimes H^{0}(K_{C})\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N)
.

Then, for
V_{2}\in\mathbb{G}
general, the map
\mu_{V_{2}}
is injective.

Proof of Claim
4.35
.

The proof is straightforward, similar to
[
12
, Claim 7.12]
. Notice first that a general point
[V_{2}]\in\mathbb{G}
determines a
2
-dimensional sub-vector space of
H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
giving rise to a base-point-free linear pencil on
C
; indeed, since
h^{0}(K_{C}-N)=3g-3-d\geqslant 4
, we can take
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in H^{0}(K_{C}-N)
general sections. If
p\in C
is such that
\sigma_{i}(p)=0
, for
i=1,2
, by the generality of the sections we would have

p\in{\rm Bs}(|K_{C}-N|)
so
h^{0}(N+p)=1
. This is a contradiction because
N
is general and
\mbox{deg}(N)=d-2g+2\leq g-5
, from the bounds on
d
. The injectivity of
\mu_{V_{2}}
follows from the base-point-free pencil trick since
\operatorname{ker}(\mu_{V_{2}})\cong H^{0}(N)
, which is zero since
N
is non-effective.
∎

Claim 4.36
.

With assumptions as above, let
N\in\operatorname{Pic}^{d-2g+2}(C)
be general. Then, there exists a unique irreducible component
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
of
\mathcal{W}_{2}\subsetneq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N)
whose general
v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is such that
{\rm Coker}(\partial_{v})^{\vee}
corresponds to a general point in the Grassmannian
\mathbb{G}
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*}
is unirational, of the expected dimension as in (
3.2
), namely
\operatorname{dim}(\mathcal{W}_{2}^{*})=9g-15-3d
.

Proof of Claim
4.36
.

Set
\mathbb{P}:=\mathbb{P}({\rm Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N))=\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(2K_{C}-N)^{\vee})
, thus any element
[\pi]\in\mathbb{P}
gives rise to a hyperplane
\pi\subset H^{0}(2K_{C}-N)
. Consider the incidence variety

\mathcal{J}:=\left\{(V_{2},\pi)\in\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{P}\;|\;{\rm im}(\mu_{V_{2}})\subseteq\pi\right\},

where
\mu_{V_{2}}:V_{2}\otimes H^{0}(K_{C})\to H^{0}(2K_{C}-N)
as in Claim
4.35
, where we noticed that for
[V_{2}]\in\mathbb{G}
general one has also
{\rm ker}(\mu_{V_{2}})=\{0\}
. From the computations above, we know that
m=\operatorname{dim}({\rm Ext}^{1}(\omega_{C},N))=5g-5-d
,
\ell=h^{0}(K)=g
, and
r=h^{0}(K_{C}-N)=3g-3-d
, so one has
\ell\geq r>2
and
m\geq 2\ell+1
. Since, from Claim
4.35
, for
[V_{2}]\in\mathbb{G}
general one has that
\mu_{V_{2}}
is injective, in particular
\operatorname{dim}({\rm im}(\mu_{V_{2}}))=2\ell\leq m-1
, i.e. for
[V_{2}]\in\mathbb{G}
general

{\rm im}(\mu_{V_{2}})
must be contained in at least one hyperplane
\pi
of
H^{0}(2K_{C}-N)
. This implies that the incidence variety
\mathcal{J}
is not empty and, by generality, it dominates
\mathbb{G}
which is of dimension
\operatorname{dim}(\mathbb{G})=2(r-2)=2(3g-5-d)=6g-10-2d
.

Consider the two natural projections
